# Parity matrix

Java file → ported/partial/not-needed/deferred. Populated per PR from Phase 1.
Pinned Lucene version: **10.5.0** (matches OpenSearch `gradle/libs.versions.toml`).

## lucene-util

| Java | Rust | Status |
|---|---|---|
| `util/BitUtil.zigZagEncode/Decode` | `lucene-util/src/zigzag.rs` | ported, fixture-verified |
| `Long.toString/parseLong(_, 36)` (generation ↔ base-36 filename suffix) | `lucene-util/src/base36.rs` | ported, round-trip tested |
| `util/FixedBitSet` (dense bitset: get/set/clear/cardinality) | `lucene-util/src/fixed_bit_set.rs::{get, set, clear, get_doc, cardinality, from_words}` | ported (the subset `.liv` reading needs); no `SparseFixedBitSet` (in-memory-only optimization, not a format difference). **Deliberate divergence (`c41-gates-and-record`): `get`/`set`/`clear` check their bound in release as well as debug.** Java carries it as an `assert`, off in production, and this port ported the `assert` -- so an index past `num_bits` but inside the final word read or wrote a *ghost bit*, a silently wrong live/dead answer no caller could detect. It now panics (containable: `lucene_ffi`'s `guard` reports `FfiStatus::Panic`), through a `#[cold]` out-of-line helper so the hot loops pay one never-taken branch. **Added, no Java counterpart: `get_doc(doc: i32)`** -- the sanctioned way to ask a live-docs/accept-ords bitset about an id produced elsewhere; it answers `false` for a negative id (Java's `as usize` sign-extension trap) and for one past the bitset, where Java's `Bits.get` throws. 30 hand-written copies of that bound were migrated onto it. See [`docs/mechanical-gates.md`](mechanical-gates.md)'s `fixed-bitset-bound` rule. |
| `util/NumericUtils.sortableFloatBits`/`floatToSortableInt`-style float helpers | `lucene-util/src/numeric_utils.rs` | ported, **float half only**. The `long`/`double` and `*ToSortableBytes` halves are still duplicated in `lucene_index::segment_info` and `lucene_search::facets` -- deduplicating them is a `docs/sweep/m2/LEDGER.md` item. Moved here from `lucene-codecs` by the c10 sweep batch. |
| `java.util.SplittableRandom` (a JDK class, not a Lucene one) | `lucene-util/src/splittable_random.rs` | **ported bit-exactly, and it has to be**: `HnswGraphBuilder` draws every node's level from this stream, so a port drawing from any other stream cannot be compared against Java's graph node for node -- which is how c5/c10 verified the graph arc for arc. Root stream only, no `split()`. |
| `util/hnsw`'s neighbour heap (`NeighborQueue`'s backing long-heap) | `lucene-util/src/ternary_long_heap.rs` | ported; the packed `(score, node)` long heap HNSW search and diversity pruning run on. Moved here from `lucene-codecs` by the c10 sweep batch. |

## lucene-analysis

| Java | Rust | Status |
|---|---|---|
| `analysis/Analyzer`/`Tokenizer`/`TokenFilter` pipeline shape | `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs::{Token, tokenize, LowerCaseFilter, StopFilter, Analyzer}` | **ported, scoped down; `tokenize()` upgraded to a real UAX#29-backed tokenizer (task #207).** `Token { term, start_offset, end_offset, position_increment, position_length }` is a plain struct standing in for real Lucene's `CharTermAttribute`/`OffsetAttribute`/`PositionIncrementAttribute` trio (no `AttributeSource` reflection machinery). `tokenize()` now delegates word segmentation to the `unicode-segmentation` crate's `unicode_word_indices()` (already a workspace dependency of `lucene-analysis` -- no new external crate was added), a compliant implementation of [UAX #29](https://www.unicode.org/reports/tr29/)'s `Word_Break` rule set, standing in for real `StandardTokenizer`'s JFlex-generated UAX#29 grammar. **Covered by this**: combining diacritical marks (a base char + combining marks, e.g. decomposed `"cafe\u{0301}"`, stays one token via UAX#29's `Extend` rule, not two), CJK ideograph segmentation (each Han ideograph is its own token -- no dictionary-based word clustering across CJK, matching real `StandardTokenizer`'s un-augmented behavior), Hangul syllable clustering (precomposed syllables are single codepoints already; conjoining Jamo sequences, e.g. U+1100 U+1161 U+11A8, cluster into one token per syllable block via UAX#29's dedicated Hangul rules), and midword punctuation (UAX#29's `MidLetter`/`MidNumLet`/`MidNum` rules, WB6/7/11/12 -- these are the *real* rules behind what was previously a 4-character hand-rolled lookup table (`is_word_connector`, now removed): embedded numeric punctuation (`"3.14"`, `"1,000"` stay one token), acronym-style internal periods (`"U.S.A."` -> `"U.S.A"`, trailing period still splits since nothing alphanumeric follows), and internal apostrophes (`"don't"`, `"O'Brien"`) all still hold, now backed by the actual algorithm rather than an approximation of it). **Still deliberately out of scope, stated explicitly** (see `tokenize()`'s own doc comment): emoji/ZWJ *sequence* grouping as a single visual glyph for output purposes (a lone emoji or ZWJ sequence contains no alphanumeric codepoints, so -- like any other non-alphanumeric run -- it simply produces no token, same as before; full grapheme-cluster-aware emoji tokenization would not need a new crate, since `unicode-segmentation` also implements UAX#29 grapheme clusters via `graphemes()`, but is out of scope since this tokenizer only ever emits alphanumeric-containing segments as terms); Lucene's own URL/email/host-name JFlex extensions layered on top of base UAX#29 (an email/URL still splits into its alphanumeric-run pieces); and UAX#29 §5.3's optional locale-specific tailoring (e.g. dictionary-based Southeast Asian word segmentation for Thai/Lao/Khmer/Myanmar). **Offset unit (c33): `Token`'s `start_offset`/`end_offset` are UTF-16 code units — Java `char` indices, exactly what `OffsetAttribute` reports.** They were UTF-8 byte offsets until c33 (harmless only while every fixture string was ASCII, and shipped into `.pos`/`.pay`/`.tvd` once c23 made `IndexWriter` write offsets); `tokenize` now converts the segmenter's byte indices in one running pass, with a whole-text `is_ascii` fast path so the common case pays nothing per token, and `Analyzer::keyword` ends its single token at the Java `char` count. Pinned by `GenAnalysis`' `utf16_*` cases over text separating UTF-8 bytes, Unicode scalars and UTF-16 code units, with negative controls asserting each case defeats both wrong units. `LowerCaseFilter` and `StopFilter` are both ported in full, including `StopFilter`'s position-increment-preservation rule: a removed stopword's own increment is carried onto the next surviving token rather than dropped, so position gaps across query slop (`PhraseQuery`/`SpanNear`) survive stopword removal. `Analyzer::standard(stopwords)` composes tokenizer + lowercase + optional stopwords, standing in for `StandardAnalyzer`. **Stopword list completeness audit (revisit, real gap found and fixed)**: `StopFilter` itself always took a caller-supplied `HashSet<String>` -- there was no built-in default English stop list anywhere in the crate, unlike real Lucene's `EnglishAnalyzer.ENGLISH_STOP_WORDS_SET` (a fixed, well-known 33-word list). Added `ENGLISH_STOP_WORDS: &[&str]`/`english_stop_words() -> HashSet<String>` reproducing that exact 33-word list (`a, an, and, are, as, at, be, but, by, for, if, in, into, is, it, no, not, of, on, or, such, that, the, their, then, there, these, they, this, to, was, will, with`), stored lowercase to match `StopFilter`'s exact case-sensitive match against already-lowercased terms. Verified with a test (`english_stop_words_matches_real_lucene_canonical_list`) that independently re-transcribes the canonical 33 words (not copy-pasted from the source list) and checks both directions -- every canonical word present, and no extra/wrong entries -- plus a lowercase-storage check and a false-positive check against representative content words (`search`/`lucene`/`rust`/`document`/`index`/`query`) run through the real `StopFilter`/`Analyzer::standard` pipeline. **Deferred**: any `TokenFilter` besides `AsciiFoldingFilter`/`PorterStemFilter`/`SynonymFilter` (see the rows below), plus per-field analyzer configuration. This crate is a standalone crate below both `lucene-index` and `lucene-search` in the workspace graph (its one external dependency, `unicode-segmentation`, is a pure Unicode-tables crate with no further workspace-graph implications); `lucene-search/src/query_parser.rs` (task #62) and `lucene-index/src/indexing_chain.rs::invert_documents` (task #63, in-memory-only builder, see that row in the `lucene-index` section) are now its two real consumers; term-vector fixtures still take already-tokenized terms directly. **Verified against real Lucene**: `crates/lucene-analysis/tests/analysis_fixtures.rs` runs real `StandardAnalyzer` with a real stopword set (`fixtures/src/GenAnalysis.java`) over six cases — a stopword mid-sentence, at the very start, at the very end, three consecutive stopwords, an all-stopwords string (empty output), and a mixed-case/punctuation sentence exercising tokenizing, lowercasing, and stopword removal together ("The" is itself a stopword once lowercased) — and asserts this port's `Analyzer` produces byte-identical (term, position_increment, start_offset, end_offset) sequences; all six passed on the first real-Lucene run. **Task #207 additionally added** five `uax29_*` cases to the same generator, running bare real `StandardTokenizer` (no filters) over combining-mark, CJK-ideograph, precomposed-Hangul, conjoining-Jamo-Hangul, and mixed-CJK/Latin text, asserted in a new `tokenize_matches_real_standard_tokenizer_on_uax29_cases` test (after the same char-to-byte offset reconciliation `ascii_folding_matches_real_ascii_folding_filter` already uses) — all five passed on the first real-Lucene run. Coverage: `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs` 99.93% lines (`cargo llvm-cov -p lucene-analysis`). **Second-language stopword list added (task #220, breadth, not the stopword-list-completeness audit itself -- that prior task already confirmed `ENGLISH_STOP_WORDS` matches real Lucene)**: this crate previously shipped default stopwords for exactly one language (English). Added `FRENCH_STOP_WORDS: &[&str]`/`french_stop_words() -> HashSet<String>`, the same 154-word list as real Lucene's `org.apache.lucene.analysis.fr.FrenchAnalyzer.getDefaultStopSet()` (itself loaded from `french_stop.txt`, a Snowball-project resource under `org/apache/lucene/analysis/snowball/` -- comment-stripped and whitespace-split the same way real Lucene's `WordlistLoader.getSnowballWordSet` does). Transcribed directly from that resource file in a real `/home/tuong/work/lucene` 10.5.0 checkout, not from memory; cross-checked programmatically against an independent extraction of the same file, confirming an exact 154-word match (including several classic French function words -- `son`, `sommes`, `été`, `est`, `avions`, `auras`, `aura`, `as`, `fût` -- that real Lucene's own file comments deliberately omit as homonyms of unrelated common words, so this is not simply "every French function word"). **Scope, stated explicitly**: this is the default stopword *list* only, wired through this crate's existing, language-agnostic `StopFilter` -- it does not add French elision (`ElisionFilter`) or French stemming (`FrenchLightStemmer`/`SnowballFilter`), both of which real Lucene's `FrenchAnalyzer` also performs; no composed `Analyzer::french()` pipeline is provided. **Verified against real Lucene**: a new `fixtures/src/GenAnalysis.java` case (`french_stopwords`) runs real `StandardTokenizer` + `LowerCaseFilter` + a real `StopFilter` fed `FrenchAnalyzer.getDefaultStopSet()` directly (not the full `FrenchAnalyzer`, matching this port's scope) over "Le chat et la souris sont dans la maison" (five stopwords interleaved with three content words); a new test, `french_stop_words_match_real_french_analyzer_default_stop_set` in `crates/lucene-analysis/tests/analysis_fixtures.rs`, asserts this port's `french_stop_words()` fed through `tokenize`/`LowerCaseFilter`/`StopFilter` produces byte-identical (term, position_increment, offset-span) output to real Lucene, including position-increment carry-over across the run of stopwords -- passed on the first real-Lucene run. Three new unit tests additionally check the exact 154-entry count plus a canonical-word sample, lowercase storage, and no false positives on French content words. Coverage: `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs` 99.32% lines (`cargo llvm-cov --workspace --summary-only`). **M2 sweep `b8-automata-analysis`:** `LowerCaseFilter` now uses Java's *simple* per-codepoint `Character.toLowerCase` (`CharacterUtils.toLowerCase`) instead of Rust's full `str::to_lowercase`. The two disagree on real text -- `U+0130 İ` gave `i` + a combining dot above instead of a bare `i`, and `"ΟΔΟΣ"` lowercased to `"οδος"` where Java gives `"οδοσ"` (Java has no final-sigma rule) -- so those terms indexed under different bytes than Lucene's. Pinned by `GenAnalysis`' `lowercase_*` fixture cases. Still open, structural: there is no `TokenStream.end()` hook, so `StopFilter`/n-gram trailing position increments are dropped rather than published. `StandardTokenizer`'s `maxTokenLength` (255) and its emoji token type are also unported. |
| `analysis/miscellaneous/ASCIIFoldingFilter` | `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs::{AsciiFoldingFilter, Analyzer::with_ascii_folding}` | **ported (task #64), scoped to a documented character subset, not the full real table.** A fourth `lucene-analysis` filter (alongside `tokenize`/`LowerCaseFilter`/`StopFilter` from task #61), folding each token's `term` to its closest plain-ASCII form. **Covered, itemized**: the full Latin-1 Supplement letter block (U+00C0-U+00DE uppercase / U+00E0-U+00FE lowercase, i.e. À-Þ/à-þ, skipping the `×`/`÷` math symbols at U+00D7/U+00F7), plus a documented Latin Extended-A subset: Polish (Ą/ą, Ć/ć, Ę/ę, Ł/ł, Ń/ń, Ś/ś, Ź/ź, Ż/ż) and Czech/Slovak/Baltic caron forms (Š/š, Č/č, Ž/ž, Ď/ď, Ť/ť, Ň/ň). `Æ`/`æ` and `Œ`/`œ` fold to **two** ASCII characters (`AE`/`ae`, `OE`/`oe` respectively — real Lucene's actual multi-char folding), and `ß` folds to `ss` (real Lucene's actual special case, not "b"/"beta") — both verified against real `ASCIIFoldingFilter` output, not assumed. **Deferred, real follow-on work**: the rest of real Lucene's table (remaining Latin Extended-A/B, Latin Extended Additional such as precomposed Vietnamese, and non-Latin-script folding e.g. fullwidth Latin forms) — a character outside this table passes through **unchanged**, never dropped, never a panic. **Offsets are never adjusted for folding-driven length changes**: folding `æther` → `aether` grows the term from 5 to 6 characters, but `start_offset`/`end_offset` still denote the *original* source text span, matching real Lucene's `ASCIIFoldingFilter`, which never touches `OffsetAttribute`. **Filter-ordering decision**: `Analyzer::with_ascii_folding()` inserts folding *before* lowercasing (tokenize → fold → lowercase → stopwords) — an uppercase accented letter (`É`) folds straight to its ASCII form (`E`) and is lowercased in the same subsequent pass as every other token, and stopword matching (last in the chain) always sees the fully folded-and-lowercased form. Folding is **off by default**: `Analyzer::standard(stopwords)` is unchanged, so `query_parser.rs` (task #62) and `indexing_chain.rs` (task #63) keep their exact prior behavior — callers opt in via the additive `.with_ascii_folding()` builder method. **Verified against real Lucene**: `fixtures/src/GenAnalysis.java` gained two new cases via a hand-built `Analyzer` subclass wiring real `StandardTokenizer` + `ASCIIFoldingFilter` — `fold_only` (fold with no lowercasing, over "café naïve Müller cœur straße") and `fold_then_lower` (the same plus real `LowerCaseFilter`, over "Café Naïve ÉCOLE") — and `crates/lucene-analysis/tests/analysis_fixtures.rs` asserts this port's `AsciiFoldingFilter`/`Analyzer::with_ascii_folding()` produce the same (term, position_increment, offset-span) sequences, both passing on the first real-Lucene run. **No offset-unit reconciliation happens in the test any more** (c33): both sides are Java `char`s, so every expectation is compared verbatim — the conversion helper that used to sit here is gone, since a reconciliation step is where a wrong unit hides. Unit tests cover: each Latin-1 spot-check (café/naïve/Müller/ñ), the eszett special case, a ligature growing the term's character count while offsets stay put, a plain-ASCII token passing through untouched, mixed diacritic+ASCII in one token, a non-table character (Cyrillic) passing through unchanged, the composed fold-then-lowercase order, and the unchanged no-folding default. Coverage: `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs` 100% lines. **M2 sweep `b8-automata-analysis`: the table is now complete** -- all **1242** mappings, generated by folding every BMP codepoint through the real `ASCIIFoldingFilter.foldToASCII` from `lucene-analysis-common-10.5.0.jar` rather than hand-transcribed. The previous 92-entry subset was 7% of Lucene's, so Vietnamese, fullwidth CJK-Latin, ligature and typographic-quote text folded to itself here and to ASCII there. `preserveOriginal` is now supported (`apply_preserving_original`). Pinned per Unicode block by `GenAnalysis`' `fold_*` fixture cases. |
| `analysis/en/PorterStemFilter` | `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs::{PorterStemFilter, Analyzer::with_stemming}` | **ported in full (task #65) -- all five steps of the classic 1980 Porter algorithm, not a subset.** A fifth `lucene-analysis` filter (alongside `tokenize`/`LowerCaseFilter`/`StopFilter`/`AsciiFoldingFilter`), stemming each token's `term` per Martin Porter's original suffix-stripping algorithm. **Steps ported, itemized**: step 1a (`-sses`->`-ss`, `-ies`->`-i`, `-ss`->`-ss` no-op, else trailing `-s`-> delete); step 1b (`-eed`->`-ee` if `m(stem) > 0`; `-ed`/`-ing` deleted only if the stem contains a vowel, followed by `-at`/`-bl`/`-iz`-append, double-consonant-not-l/s/z-drop, or `m==1`-and-CVC-append cleanup); step 1c (trailing `-y`->`-i` if the stem contains a vowel); step 2, `m(stem) > 0` (the full 20-entry suffix-family table, `-ational`->`-ate` through `-biliti`->`-ble`, tried longest/most-specific-suffix-first exactly as the original paper orders them); step 3, `m(stem) > 0` (`-icate`->`-ic`, `-ative`-> delete, `-alize`->`-al`, `-iciti`->`-ic`, `-ical`->`-ic`, `-ful`/`-ness`-> delete); step 4, `m(stem) > 1` (removes `-al`/`-ance`/`-ence`/`-er`/`-ic`/`-able`/`-ible`/`-ant`/`-ement`/`-ment`/`-ent`/`-ion` (only if the stem ends in `s`/`t`)/`-ou`/`-ism`/`-ate`/`-iti`/`-ous`/`-ive`/`-ize`); step 5a (trailing `-e` deleted if `m(stem) > 1`, or if `m(stem) == 1` and the stem is not CVC); step 5b (a trailing double `-ll` collapses to a single `-l` if `m(word) > 1`). Nothing is deferred within the algorithm itself -- this is the complete classic port. **Domain of definition, explicit**: the algorithm (and real Lucene's own port of it) is defined only over lowercase ASCII alphabetic words; a term with any non-ASCII-alphabetic character or uppercase letter passes through **unchanged**, never a panic -- a non-issue in the normal chain since this filter runs after `LowerCaseFilter`. **Filter-ordering decision**: `Analyzer::with_stemming()` inserts stemming *last* (tokenize -> fold -> lowercase -> stopwords -> stem), matching real Lucene's `EnglishAnalyzer` (its stop set holds unstemmed words like `"the"`, so stopword matching must see pre-stem terms). Off by default -- existing callers unaffected; opt in via the additive `.with_stemming()` builder method. **Verification**: direct unit tests against known Porter-algorithm input/output pairs, not a new Java fixture -- this is a purely algorithmic, non-file-format task (no on-disk bytes to decode), and the test vocabulary is drawn from the algorithm's own canonical worked examples (Porter's 1980 paper's step 2-4 illustration list, e.g. `"relational"`->`"relat"`, `"operator"`->`"oper"`, `"triplicate"`->`"triplic"`, plus the paper's separate step 1a/1b/1c illustration list, e.g. `"caresses"`->`"caress"`, `"agreed"`->`"agre"`, `"hopping"`->`"hop"`, `"happy"`->`"happi"`), independently traced step-by-step against this implementation rather than guessed. Unit tests cover: step 1a plurals (caresses/ponies/ties/caress/cats), step 1b's `m`/vowel guards including words that must **not** stem (feed/bled/sing all fail their respective conditions and stay unchanged; agreed->agre, plastered->plaster, motoring->motor do stem) plus each of its three post-deletion cleanup branches individually (`-at`/`-bl`/`-iz`-append via sized->size; double-consonant-drop via hopping->hop/tanned->tan/falling->fall/hissing->hiss/fizzed->fizz; plain deletion via failing->fail/filing->file), step 1c (happy->happi vs. sky->sky, whose vowel guard doesn't fire), the full step 2/3/4 suffix-family table (47 canonical pairs), step 5's final-e/double-l edge cases (`rate` keeps its `e` since `m==1` and it is CVC; `roll` keeps one `l` since `m==1` not `>1`), offsets/position-increment left untouched, non-lowercase-ASCII pass-through (uppercase, accented, empty, digit-only terms), and the composed `Analyzer::with_stemming()` running after `StopFilter`. Coverage: `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs` 99.11% lines. **Porter2/Snowball parity check (revisit, no code change to the algorithm)**: reread against the Snowball English ("Porter2") algorithm spec to check for R1/R2-region gaps -- confirmed this port's scope target (real Lucene's `EnglishAnalyzer`, which wires up `PorterStemFilter`) genuinely runs the **classic 1980 Porter algorithm** in real Lucene too (`org.tartarus.snowball.ext.PorterStemmer`, generated from Snowball's `porter.sbl`), not Porter2/`english.sbl` -- so there is no missing R1/R2 region computation to add *here*, since R1/R2 belongs to a different algorithm this filter was never scoped to. **Explicitly deferred, real gap**: the Porter2/"Snowball English" stemmer (`org.tartarus.snowball.ext.EnglishStemmer`, exposed in real Lucene only via `SnowballFilter` constructed with the `English` variant -- a *different, non-default* filter `EnglishAnalyzer` does not use) is **not ported at all** -- its own step 0 (apostrophe-suffix removal), R1/R2 region computation threaded through steps 2-5, doubled-consonant undoubling, and per-step exception tables are a separate, sizeable algorithm and a legitimate follow-on task, not a subset or superset of the `PorterStemFilter` row above. A smaller-scoped alternative was also considered and rejected for now, not silently skipped: adding Porter2/Snowball-English as a new, separate, opt-in sibling filter (leaving `PorterStemFilter` untouched) is real and tractable, but its own R1/R2-threading, step 0, doubled-consonant undoubling, and exception table are enough surface area to warrant their own dedicated task rather than a same-task add-on here. One new test, `porter_stem_step1a_step1b_step1c_paper_vocabulary`, closes the step 1a/1b/1c vocabulary-coverage gap that check surfaced; `cargo test -p lucene-analysis`: 54 lib tests pass (up from 53). **Porter2/Snowball English stemmer ported (task #209, closes the gap above)**: added `SnowballEnglishStemFilter`/`Analyzer::with_snowball_stemming()` as a new, separate, opt-in fifth filter alongside `PorterStemFilter` (not touching it) -- exactly the "smaller-scoped alternative" the task #175 revisit above weighed and, at the time, deferred. Implements real Snowball's `org.tartarus.snowball.ext.EnglishStemmer` (generated from `english.sbl`), the algorithm real Lucene exposes only via `SnowballFilter` constructed with an `EnglishStemmer` -- a different, non-default filter `EnglishAnalyzer` does not wire up. **Ported, itemized**: step 0 (leading-apostrophe stripping in the prelude, plus trailing `'`/`'s`/`'s'` removal folded into step 1a exactly as the real generated code combines them); the `y`/`Y` consonant-vs-vowel bookkeeping (a leading `y`, or any `y` immediately following a vowel, is treated as a consonant for the rest of the algorithm, reverted to lowercase `y` in the postlude); R1/R2 region computation, including the three irregular-prefix words (`arsenal`, `commune`, `generalization`-family) whose R1 is forced to start right after the fixed prefix; steps 1a-1c, 2, 3, 4, 5; the short-syllable (`r_shortv`) test; and two whole-word exception tables -- one checked *before* anything else (`skis`->`ski`, `skies`->`sky`, `dying`->`die`, `lying`->`lie`, `tying`->`tie`, `idly`->`idl`, `gently`->`gentl`, `ugly`->`ugli`, `early`->`earli`, `only`->`onli`, `singly`->`singl`, plus `andes`/`atlas`/`bias`/`cosmos`/`howe`/`news`/`sky` left unchanged), one checked after step 1a but before step 1b (`succeed`/`proceed`/`exceed`/`canning`/`inning`/`earring`/`herring`/`outing`, all left completely unchanged, skipping steps 1b-5 entirely). **A real, non-obvious gap this task closed while implementing**: the actual algorithm shipped in real Lucene 10.5.0's `lucene-analysis-common-10.5.0.jar` (decompiled directly from that jar via `javap`/CFR, since it is the authoritative artifact real code links against) turned out to disagree with the `EnglishStemmer.java` source file found in a `/home/tuong/work/lucene` git checkout -- that checkout is a materially different (evidently newer/upstream) generation of `english.sbl`, with nine R1-override prefixes instead of three, no `r_exception2` step, and a different `-ing`-family special case. The two disagree on real words (`"organization"` -> `"organ"` vs. `"organiz"`; `"emergency"` -> `"emerg"` vs. `"emergenc"`); this port matches the jar, confirmed by directly instrumenting a decompiled copy of the jar's actual class with debug prints and cross-checking against `SnowballFilter`'s real output for both words. **Domain of definition**: lowercase ASCII letters plus a literal `'` (step 0 needs it for `"don't"`/`"cats'"`/`"'tis"`); anything else passes through unchanged, never a panic. **Filter-ordering / wiring**: `Analyzer::with_snowball_stemming()` occupies the same chain position as `with_stemming()` (tokenize -> fold -> lowercase -> stopwords -> stem); if both are enabled on one `Analyzer`, the Snowball stemmer takes precedence (no double-stemming -- not a real Lucene configuration either filter models). **Verified against real Lucene**: a new `fixtures/src/GenAnalysis.java` case (`snowball_english`) runs real `StandardTokenizer` + `LowerCaseFilter` + `SnowballFilter(new EnglishStemmer())` over a 112-word list covering every step/table above (including apostrophe/possessive cases, the R1-irregular-prefix words, both exception tables, and known step-2/3/4/5 chaining edge cases like `"emergency"`->`"emerg"`, `"organization"`->`"organ"`, `"generalization"`->`"general"`, `"trembly"`->`"trembl"`); a new test, `snowball_english_stemmer_matches_real_snowball_english_stemmer` in `crates/lucene-analysis/tests/analysis_fixtures.rs`, asserts byte-identical (term, position_increment, offset) output against that fixture -- passed on the first real-Lucene run after the jar-vs-checkout discrepancy above was found and corrected for. 18 additional unit tests cover each algorithm branch directly (prelude apostrophe/y-marking, both exception tables, the `aeo`-doubled-consonant carve-out, the short-word append-`e` fallback, each suffix family, and non-domain/short-word passthrough). Coverage: `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs` 99.59% lines (`cargo llvm-cov -p lucene-analysis`); the two uncovered lines are pre-existing classic-Porter code and one step-3 `tional` table entry that is provably unreachable given step 2's identical, identically-R1-gated `tional`/`ational` handling runs first -- the same structural redundancy real Snowball's own generated code carries. **M2 sweep `b8-automata-analysis`: four correctness fixes**, all pinned by a new `porter_english` differential fixture over a 100-word vocabulary. (1) Java runs no step at all unless `k > k0 + 1`, i.e. the word is at least three characters -- without that guard `"s"` stemmed to the **empty string** and `"as"`/`"is"`/`"us"` lost their `s`. (2) Java has no lowercase-ASCII precondition, so `"Cats"` really does stem to `"Cat"`; this port used to pass such words through. (3) `bli -> ble` was written as `abli -> able` and the `logi -> log` rule was missing outright. (4) Java `switch`es on one character and `break`s at the **first** suffix whose `ends()` succeeds, *before* `r()` tests `m()`, so a measure failure stops the search rather than falling through to a shorter suffix -- `"argument"` stays `"argument"` in Lucene where a fall-through gives `"argum"`. **Offset unit (c33)**: the filter never touches `OffsetAttribute`, so a stem that shortens the term keeps the *source* span -- now in Java `char`s, pinned by `GenAnalysis`' `utf16_porter_after_astral` case behind a supplementary-plane token (where UTF-8 bytes, Unicode scalars and UTF-16 code units all differ). |
| `analysis/synonym/SynonymFilter`/`SynonymGraphFilter` | `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs::{SynonymFilter, Analyzer::with_synonyms, Analyzer::with_bidirectional_synonyms}` | **ported (task #66), scoped to single-word-to-single-word synonyms only -- not the full `SynonymGraphFilter`.** A fifth `lucene-analysis` filter (alongside `tokenize`/`LowerCaseFilter`/`StopFilter`/`AsciiFoldingFilter`/`PorterStemFilter`), taking a caller-supplied `HashMap<String, Vec<String>>` and, for each token whose term is a map key, injecting one additional token per configured replacement immediately after the original. **Explicit scope boundary**: real `SynonymGraphFilter` also handles multi-word synonym *phrases* (`"New York"` <-> `"NYC"`) via a graph token stream -- legitimately out-of-scope machinery here; this filter only maps one term to one or more single-word replacement terms. **Positional semantics (the core real-Lucene rule mirrored)**: an injected synonym gets `position_increment == 0` and the exact same `start_offset`/`end_offset` as the original -- it occupies the *same position*, an alternative reading of the same span, not a new one, so `PhraseQuery`/`SpanNear` built against either term still aligns with surrounding words. This is the first token in the crate with `position_increment == 0` (every prior token, including `StopFilter`'s carried-over increments, has been `>= 1`). **Bidirectionality is NOT automatic by default**, matching real Lucene's `SynonymMap`: configuring `"quick" -> ["fast"]` does not also expand `"fast"` to `"quick"` through the original `SynonymFilter::apply`/`Analyzer::with_synonyms` entry points -- unchanged and unaffected by the addition below. **Bidirectional expansion (follow-up task, "SynonymFilter bidirectional expansion")**: a second, purely additive opt-in entry point, `SynonymFilter::apply_bidirectional`/`Analyzer::with_bidirectional_synonyms`, mirrors real Lucene's `SynonymMap.Builder(true)` bidirectional construction mode at this filter's same single-word-to-single-word scope: given the same `HashMap<String, Vec<String>>` config, a `key -> [values]` mapping also expands each `value -> key` -- configuring only `"cat" -> ["feline"]` is enough for analyzing `"feline"` to inject `"cat"` too, without the caller configuring both directions. A private helper, `build_bidirectional_map`, builds the combined forward+reverse map once per `apply_bidirectional` call (not re-derived per token) and dedupes so a term already configured in both directions (e.g. `"cat" -> ["feline"]` and `"feline" -> ["cat"]` both present) is never injected twice. **What this does NOT replicate**: transitive closure (if `"cat" -> ["feline"]` and `"feline" -> ["kitty"]` are both configured, this does not infer `"cat" -> ["kitty"]` -- only the direct reverse of each configured pair is added), multi-word synonym phrases, weighted/scored synonyms, and real Lucene's `includeOrig` flag -- the same carve-outs the unidirectional case already states. **Filter-ordering decision**: `Analyzer::with_synonyms()`/`with_bidirectional_synonyms()` both insert synonym expansion *last* (tokenize -> fold -> lowercase -> stopwords -> stem -> synonyms) -- real Lucene's convention is that synonym expansion runs over already-normalized terms, so the caller's map keys are expected to already be lowercased/stemmed; and running after `StopFilter` means a term that is itself a stopword (and thus removed) never gets its synonym expanded, since expanding a term about to be dropped would produce an orphaned synonym token with no corresponding original. Both entry points off by default -- existing callers unaffected; opt in via `.with_synonyms(map)` (unidirectional) or `.with_bidirectional_synonyms(map)` (bidirectional). **Verification approach**: no new Java fixture -- the `position_increment == 0` injection is new Rust-level control flow over an already cross-engine-verified position system (`StopFilter`'s increment mechanics were differentially verified in task #61), not new byte-format decoding, so unit tests are the appropriate tool. Unit tests cover: one configured synonym produces two tokens (original at its own increment, synonym at increment 0); multiple synonyms produce the original plus all of them, all at increment 0; a token with no configured synonym passes through unchanged; synonym expansion is confirmed NOT automatically bidirectional through the original `apply`; injected-token offsets match the original's exactly; composed with `StopFilter` (a stopword's own synonym is never expanded since the stopword is removed first, and a surviving term's synonym is correctly carried through with the right accumulated `position_increment`); composed with `PorterStemFilter` (the synonym map's key matches the *stemmed* form, proving synonyms see post-stemming terms); a forward-only-configured term expanding both ways under `apply_bidirectional`; both-directions-configured producing no duplicate injection; and bidirectional mode composed with both `StopFilter` and `PorterStemFilter`, mirroring the unidirectional composition tests. `cargo test -p lucene-analysis`: 44 lib tests pass (up from 37). Coverage: new code (`apply_bidirectional`, `build_bidirectional_map`, `with_bidirectional_synonyms`) fully exercised by the new tests. **Multi-word phrase support (follow-up task, "Synonym expansion multi-word phrase support")**: a third, purely additive entry point, `SynonymFilter::apply_multiword(tokens, rules: &[SynonymRule])`, extends matching to multi-token input phrases (e.g. `"wi" "fi" -> "wifi"`) and multi-token output phrases (e.g. `"usa" -> "united" "states" "of" "america"`), including multi-word-to-multi-word. `SynonymRule { input: Vec<String>, outputs: Vec<Vec<String>> }` replaces the single-word `HashMap<String, Vec<String>>` shape for this entry point only (`apply`/`apply_bidirectional` and their `HashMap` config are completely unchanged). Matching requires lookahead across multiple input tokens (buffered via direct indexing into the input `Vec<Token>`, not the prior per-token loop): at each starting position, every candidate rule sharing that position's term as its first input word is tried longest-first (greedy longest match), and a rule only fires when every one of its input words matches the corresponding contiguous following token -- a bare prefix match (`"wi"` not followed by `"fi"`, including `"wi"` as the last token with nothing following) never fires. **`position_length` added to `Token`** (new field, default `1` everywhere else in the crate) to carry real Lucene's `PositionLengthAttribute`: a multi-word-input/single-word-output match emits one token at `position_increment == 0` and `position_length == ` the number of input tokens it replaces; a multi-word output phrase emits one chained token per output word (first at `position_increment == 0`, the rest at `1` each, all at `position_length == 1`). **Scope, stated explicitly**: this is real `SynonymGraphFilter`'s token-stream *shape* (graph-relevant attributes recorded on ordinary `Token`s) proven via direct token-stream inspection, not its full graph `TokenStream`/lattice-traversal API -- there is no graph-aware traversal type, output is still one flat `Vec<Token>`, and a multi-word *output* phrase does not extend the overall position count the way a true lattice would (tokens after the match keep the position they'd have had relative to the original single input position). Consuming this correctly from a real `PhraseQuery`/`SpanQuery` lattice traversal is deferred; not wired into `Analyzer` (no `with_multiword_synonyms` builder method) since the flat-consumer approximation isn't yet proven against a real downstream query consumer. Covered by new unit tests: multi-word input to single-word output (`"wi fi"` -> `"wifi"`, `position_length == 2`), single-word input to multi-word output (`"usa"` -> 4 chained tokens), multi-word-to-multi-word (`"new york"` -> `"big apple"`), a non-matching partial prefix (both mid-stream and at end-of-stream), no-rules passthrough, longest-match preference when a shorter and longer rule could both match the same start, multiple alternative output paths for one input phrase, and a regression test confirming `apply`/`apply_bidirectional`'s existing single-word behavior is byte-identical after this addition. **M2 sweep `b8-automata-analysis`:** `apply_multiword` is now `SynonymGraphFilter.bufferOutputTokens` node for node, pinned by six `GenAnalysis` `syn_*` fixture cases. Two divergences fixed: the collapsed synonym was emitted *after* the originals with increment 0, putting it one position too late (`wifi` at `fi`'s position rather than `wi`'s); and every token, originals included, was stamped with the whole match's offsets, producing a **decreasing** `startOffset` that real Lucene's `IndexingChain` rejects outright. A single input expanding to a multi-word output now also gives the original the right `position_length`. Still scoped out: `keepOrig=false` (the Solr `=>` form), and nothing in this port consumes the graph attributes the way `GraphTokenFilter` does. **Offset unit (c33)**: every offset here is copied from an input token (a collapsed match spans the first token's `startOffset` to the last one's `endOffset`), so the unit change needed no code change -- but it is exactly the kind of edit that could regress b8's non-decreasing-`startOffset` fix, so `GenAnalysis`' `utf16_syn_multiword` case pins the whole graph in Java `char`s between a supplementary-plane and a CJK token, with the non-decreasing rule asserted directly. |
| `analysis/ngram/NGramTokenFilter`/`EdgeNGramTokenFilter` | `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs::{NGramTokenFilter, EdgeNGramTokenFilter, ngrams_for_term, apply_ngram_filter}` | **ported, token-filter form only -- the tokenizer forms (`NGramTokenizer`/`EdgeNGramTokenizer`) are a real, deferred gap, stated explicitly below.** A sixth/seventh `lucene-analysis` filter, gramming each already-tokenized token's `term` into substrings. `NGramTokenFilter::apply(tokens, min_gram, max_gram)` produces every contiguous codepoint substring whose length is in `min_gram..=max_gram`, ordered by increasing start position then increasing length (`"abcde"` with min=2/max=3 -> `"ab","abc","bc","bcd","cd","cde","de"`, confirmed exact order by test). `EdgeNGramTokenFilter::apply` is the same algorithm restricted to grams anchored at codepoint 0 (prefix-only, the autocomplete/prefix-search shape; `"abcde"` with min=2/max=4 -> `"ab","abc","abcd"`). Both share one internal implementation (`ngrams_for_term`/`apply_ngram_filter`) parameterized by an `edge_only` flag, avoiding near-duplicate logic. **Token-filter vs. tokenizer form, the scope decision**: real Lucene has both a tokenizer form (grams raw text directly, ignoring word boundaries) and a token-filter form (grams already-tokenized terms). Only the token-filter form is implemented here, since it composes naturally with this crate's existing `Vec<Token> -> Vec<Token>` filter-chain shape (same as every other filter in the crate) and lets n-gramming run downstream of `tokenize()`'s word-boundary logic. The tokenizer form -- a raw-`&str -> Vec<Token>` entry point that grams straight across whitespace/punctuation -- is **not implemented**; a real, tractable follow-on task, not silently dropped. **Edge cases**: a token shorter than `min_gram` (in codepoints) produces **no output at all** (confirmed real Lucene behavior -- not a truncated/padded gram, not the whole token passed through); `min_gram > max_gram`, and `min_gram`/`max_gram` `<= 0`, are all rejected as `Result::Err` configuration errors (not a panic, not silently swallowed) by a shared `validate_gram_range` helper; a single-character token with `min_gram == 1` yields exactly one gram equal to the whole token; multiple input tokens are grammed fully independently (no gramming across token boundaries). **Unicode handling**: grams are computed by Unicode codepoint (via `chars().collect::<Vec<char>>()`), never by byte, so a multi-byte character (e.g. `é` in `"café"`) is never split mid-character; the emitted grams carry the **input token's** offsets verbatim (Java `restoreState`s the captured `OffsetAttribute` and never calls `setOffset`), so they are Java `char` spans of the source text, not of the gram. **Position/offset convention chosen**: the first gram derived from a given input token keeps that token's own `position_increment`; every subsequent gram from the same input token gets `position_increment == 0` (an alternative reading at the same starting position -- the same convention `SynonymFilter` already uses for injected tokens), and every gram's `position_length` stays `1`. Each gram gets its own precise `start_offset`/`end_offset` (the codepoint span it covers within the original term, translated to bytes and added onto the original token's `start_offset`), not the whole original token's span reused for every gram. **Not wired into `Analyzer`**: no `with_ngrams`/`with_edge_ngrams` builder method yet, since n-gram filters are typically used in a dedicated autocomplete/fuzzy-matching analyzer rather than the general-purpose chain this crate's `Analyzer` composes -- callers needing n-grams today call `NGramTokenFilter::apply`/`EdgeNGramTokenFilter::apply` directly on a token stream. Unit tests cover: the exact `"abcde"` min=2/max=3 gram set and order (plain) and min=2/max=4 prefix set (edge), position_increment convention (first gram gets original increment, rest get 0) for both filters, a token shorter than `min_gram` producing empty output for both filters, `min_gram > max_gram` rejected as `Err` for both filters, `min_gram`/`max_gram` `<= 0` (including negative) rejected as `Err` for both filters, a single-character token, a multi-byte Unicode token (`"café"`) confirming codepoint-based gramming and correct byte-offset translation for both filters, and multiple input tokens grammed independently (including that each token's own `position_increment` seeds its own first gram) for both filters. **M2 sweep `b8-automata-analysis`:** two correctness fixes plus `preserveOriginal`. Java accumulates `curPosIncr` across input tokens that emit no gram and only zeroes it once a gram is emitted, so a token shorter than `minGram` still consumes a position -- this port dropped that increment, shifting every later gram. And every gram carries the **input token's** offsets, because Java `restoreState`s and never calls `setOffset` (its javadoc says so, and says this is why highlighting is broken with it); this port computed a precise per-gram range instead, disagreeing with Lucene on every gram. `apply_preserving_original` adds the flag. Pinned by five `GenAnalysis` `ngram_*`/`edge_ngram_*` fixture cases. `end()`'s trailing increment is still dropped (no lifecycle hook). **Offset unit (c33)**: `restoreState` copies the input token's offsets, so the grams report Java `char` spans while still being *sliced* by code point (`Character.offsetByCodePoints`) -- the first 2-gram of `\u{1D400}bc` is 2 code points and 4 `char`s. Pinned by `GenAnalysis`' `utf16_ngram_offsets`/`utf16_edge_ngram_offsets` cases. |
| `analysis/core/KeywordAnalyzer`/`KeywordTokenizer` | `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs::Analyzer::keyword` | **ported (task #208), a second, entirely distinct `Analyzer` producer alongside `Analyzer::standard`.** Real Lucene's `KeywordAnalyzer` wires up a bare `KeywordTokenizer` with no filters at all: the entire field value becomes **exactly one token**, unmodified -- no word segmentation, no lowercasing, no stopword removal, no ASCII-folding, no stemming, no synonym expansion. `Analyzer::keyword()` reproduces this directly in `analyze()` (a short-circuit before `tokenize()`/the filter chain runs at all, gated by a private `keyword: bool` field), rather than composing it out of existing filters -- there is no filter chain to compose, by design. **Stated as intentional, not partial**: this is real `KeywordAnalyzer`'s complete, documented scope, not a cut-down `Analyzer::standard` -- it exists for exact-match/sort fields (IDs, tags, status codes) where any tokenization at all would be wrong. Calling any of `Analyzer`'s filter-chain builders (`with_ascii_folding`, `with_stemming`, `with_synonyms`, `with_bidirectional_synonyms`) on the result of `Analyzer::keyword()` has no effect on `analyze()`'s output, matching real Lucene (there is nothing for those filters to attach to). **Non-obvious edge case, verified against real Lucene rather than assumed**: empty input still produces **one** token (empty `term`, zero-length `0..0` offset span), not zero tokens -- real `KeywordTokenizer.incrementToken()` unconditionally returns `true` exactly once regardless of how many characters it read, including zero, then `false` on every subsequent call. **Verified against real Lucene**: `fixtures/src/GenAnalysis.java` gained five `keyword_*` cases (a plain id-like string, a mixed-case string with punctuation that would split under `StandardAnalyzer`, embedded leading/trailing whitespace, non-ASCII text, and the empty-string edge case) run through real `KeywordAnalyzer`; `crates/lucene-analysis/tests/analysis_fixtures.rs::keyword_analyzer_matches_real_keyword_analyzer` asserts `Analyzer::keyword()` produces byte-identical (term, position_increment, offset-span) output for all five, all passing on the first real-Lucene run. Unit tests additionally cover: whole-text-as-one-token with no splitting on internal whitespace, no lowercasing, a term that would be a stopword under `Analyzer::standard` passing through untouched, the empty-input single-empty-token case, and that every filter-chain builder method is a no-op when chained onto `Analyzer::keyword()`. Coverage: `lucene-analysis/src/lib.rs` 99.93% lines (`cargo llvm-cov -p lucene-analysis`). **Offset unit (c33)**: Java's `KeywordTokenizer` ends its single token at `correctOffset(upto)`, where `upto` counts `char`s read into a `char[]`; `Analyzer::keyword` now ends at `utf16_len(text)` instead of `text.len()`, which was the UTF-8 byte length. Pinned by `GenAnalysis`' `utf16_keyword_astral` case (`id-<emoji>-\u{E9}`: 7 `char`s, 6 scalars, 10 bytes). |

## lucene-store

| Java | Rust | Status |
|---|---|---|
| `store/DataInput` (vint/vlong/zint/zlong) | `lucene-store/src/data_input.rs` | ported, fixture-verified. `read_vint` consumes at most 5 bytes and reports a 5th continuation bit (which `writeVInt` can never emit) as an error. This is a **deliberate divergence**, corrected here by c19's Tier-2 review: 10.5.0's `DataInput.readVInt` is `for (int shift = 7; (b & 0x80) != 0; shift += 7)` -- *unbounded*, so a corrupt stream of continuation bytes reads to EOF while shifting past 32 (undefined-ish but harmless in Java, since `<<` masks the shift count). Capping it turns a corrupt-input read-to-EOF into an immediate `MalformedVarint`; see `data_input.rs`'s module doc, which states the same thing. `read_zint` (`readZInt`) added by the M2 sweep; `read_string`/`read_map_of_strings`/`read_set_of_strings` reject a negative or beyond-EOF length rather than attempting the allocation Java's `new byte[length]` would fail on |
| `util/GroupVIntUtil` (read **and** write) | `lucene-store/src/data_input.rs::read_group_vints`, `lucene-store/src/data_output.rs::write_group_vints` | ported, fixture-verified both ways: the decoder matches Java's values, and the encoder reproduces Java's 1024-value `group_vint.bin` byte-for-byte. The branchless over-read path is chosen once per group on `remaining() >= 16` (Java's `length - pos >= 4 * Integer.BYTES`), not once per value |
| `store/DataInput.readString` | `lucene-store/src/data_input.rs::read_string` | ported |
| `codecs/CodecUtil` (header/index-header/footer, CRC-32, `headerLength`/`indexHeaderLength`) | `lucene-store/src/codec_util.rs` | ported, fixture-verified (incl. corrupted-checksum case). `header_length`/`index_header_length` added by the M2 sweep, and the codec-name/suffix constraints Java enforces with `IllegalArgumentException` (ASCII, <128 / <256 bytes — the constraints that make those lengths correct) are debug-asserted |
| `codecs/CodecUtil.retrieveChecksum` (structural-only footer check, no full-file CRC), incl. the `expectedLength` overload | `lucene-store/src/codec_util.rs::{retrieve_checksum, retrieve_checksum_with_expected_length}` | ported, unit-tested |
| `store/Directory`, `FSDirectory` (listing + whole-file read) | `lucene-store/src/directory.rs::{Directory, FsDirectory}` | ported (read-only), fixture-verified |
| `store/MMapDirectory` | `lucene-store/src/directory.rs::MmapDirectory` | ported (read-only), fixture-verified; this crate's only `unsafe`. **Deliberate divergence (`c42-readpath-perf`)**: a file of at most `SMALL_FILE_READ_THRESHOLD` (16 KiB) is `fs::read` into an owned buffer rather than mapped. Java's `MMapDirectory.openInput` maps unconditionally; measured here, `open`+`mmap`+`munmap` of a few-hundred-byte file costs 1.86 us against `open`+`read`'s 1.19 us before the mapping's first page fault, and every file that small in a segment (`.si`/`.fnm`/`.tmd`/`.dvm`/`.nvm`/`.kdm`/`segments_N`) is parsed whole at open. Worth 61.9 -> 48.8 us on `DirectoryReader::open` over the 5M-document corpus. Callers see identical bytes -- `Input` already had both representations -- and `MmapDirectory::with_read_threshold(root, 0)` restores the map-everything behaviour. |
| `index/SegmentInfos.getLastCommitGeneration`, `generationFromSegmentsFileName`, `IndexFileNames.fileNameFromGeneration` | `lucene-store/src/directory.rs::{last_commit_generation, generation_from_segments_file_name, segments_file_name, read_latest_commit}` | ported, fixture-verified end-to-end (open dir → find latest commit → parse `segments_N`) |
| `store/IndexInput.slice(sliceDescription, offset, length)` / `.clone()` | `lucene-store/src/data_input.rs::SliceInput::slice_input`, `Clone for SliceInput` | **ported.** `slice_input` returns a new `SliceInput` over `[offset, offset+length)` of the parent's own addressing, cursor reset to 0, independent of the parent's and any sibling's file pointer; `Clone` (already derived) gives the same independent-pointer duplicate Java's `clone()` does, since `SliceInput` is just `(&[u8], usize)`. Slicing a slice is supported (offsets are always relative to the callee). `Directory::open` still returns a whole-file buffer rather than lazily paging a real file — that's unchanged and remains fine for this port's read-into-memory/mmap model, since `slice_input` works over either backend's bytes once opened. `crates/lucene-codecs/src/compound_format.rs::open_input` was refactored to return a `SliceInput` via this primitive instead of a hand-rolled `data.get(range)` slice, since it's the same offset/length-into-a-shared-buffer shape and the real caller (segment merging, reading one source segment's sub-range out of a shared `.cfs`) needs an independent cursor, not just a raw byte range. Unit-tested (bounds, zero-length, slice-of-a-slice, interleaved-reads independence) plus a real-on-disk-file test in `directory.rs` |
| `store/DataOutput` (vint/vlong/zint/zlong/group-vint/string/fixed-width encode) | `lucene-store/src/data_output.rs` | **write path started.** `write_zint` and `write_group_vints` added by the M2 sweep (the latter byte-verified against Java's own fixture bytes); `write_vlong` debug-asserts the non-negative contract Java enforces with `IllegalArgumentException`. Ported as an in-memory-buffer-only sink (`VecDataOutput`, plus a direct `impl DataOutput for Vec<u8>`); every codec writer built on this (stored fields, field infos, `.si`) still returns an owned `Vec<u8>` rather than targeting `IndexOutput` directly (see the row below for why that's an intentional, low-risk choice, not a gap). Unit-tested by round-tripping every primitive through `DataInput`'s decoders |
| `codecs/CodecUtil.writeHeader/writeIndexHeader/writeFooter` (encode side) | `lucene-store/src/codec_util.rs::{write_header, write_index_header, write_footer}` | ported, unit-tested by round-tripping through this port's own `check_header`/`check_index_header`/`check_footer` |
| `store/IndexOutput`/`OutputStreamIndexOutput` (sequential write to a real on-disk file) | `lucene-store/src/index_output.rs::{IndexOutput, FsIndexOutput}` | **ported.** `FsIndexOutput` wraps a real `std::fs::File` (buffered via `BufWriter`), implements `DataOutput` directly, and tracks `getFilePointer()`/`getChecksum()` (running CRC32) like Java's `OutputStreamIndexOutput`. `DataOutput`'s methods stay infallible by signature (matching `VecDataOutput`, which can never fail — making the trait fallible would ripple through every already-committed codec writer for no benefit there): a real file I/O error is latched as a **sticky error** on first failure, every subsequent write becomes a no-op, and the latched error surfaces from `close()`. `close()` flushes to the OS but deliberately does **not** fsync -- durability is `Directory::sync`'s job (next row), matching Java's actual contract that `close()` alone doesn't guarantee durability. The three existing write-path example binaries (`write_stored_fields_fixture`, `write_field_infos_fixture`, `write_segment_info_fixture`) now route their encoded bytes through this real `Directory`/`IndexOutput` instead of `std::fs::write`, and re-running each of their Java verifiers (`VerifyStoredFields`/`VerifyFieldInfos`/`VerifySegmentInfo`) against the resulting files still passes -- proving the primitive produces real, Java-`FSDirectory`-openable files, without needing new Java scaffolding for this slice |
| `store/Directory.createOutput`/`Directory.sync` (real on-disk write + fsync-before-durable contract) | `lucene-store/src/directory.rs::Directory::{create_output, sync}` (shared impl in `index_output.rs`) | **ported** for both `FsDirectory` and `MmapDirectory` (write doesn't need mmap, so both backends share the same real-file logic). `create_output` creates/truncates a file and returns an `FsIndexOutput`; `sync` fsyncs every named file's contents plus, best-effort, the containing directory entry -- mirrors `IndexWriter`'s real requirement to fsync a new segment's files before a `segments_N` commit references them. Unit-tested by round-tripping bytes written through `create_output` back through the existing read-side `Directory::open` (both backends), plus error paths (missing directory, missing file to sync, a write that fails partway through). **Deliberately out of scope for this slice**: `segments_N`/`SegmentInfos.write` (the commit file itself — generation numbers, checksum-of-checksums, atomic rename/publish) is a separate, larger piece with its own commit-lifecycle correctness risks, tracked in its own row below; so is `NativeFSLockFactory` (write-lock acquisition, not needed until concurrent-writer support matters) **Sweep b9 (M2)** added the rest of the commit-publish primitives: `Directory::{rename, delete_file, sync_meta_data}` (`Directory.rename`/`deleteFile`/`syncMetaData`), plus `pending_segments_file_name` (`IndexFileNames.fileNameFromGeneration(PENDING_SEGMENTS, ...)`). `pending_segments` deliberately does not start with `segments`, so `last_commit_generation` cannot see a half-written commit -- that invisibility is what makes `SegmentInfos.prepareCommit`/`finishCommit` crash-safe. Unit-tested: name mapping (`-1`/`0`/`1`/base-36), a pending file being invisible to the generation scan, rename-then-delete round trip through a real `FsDirectory`, and the `Err(Io)` paths for a missing rename source / delete target. `NativeFSLockFactory` (write-lock acquisition) remains out of scope. **Sweep c3 (M2)** built `IndexFileDeleter`-style reference counting on top of these primitives -- see the `index/IndexFileDeleter` row below; no further `Directory` primitive was needed. |

## lucene-codecs

| Java | Rust | Status |
|---|---|---|
| `codecs/lucene90/Lucene90LiveDocsFormat` (`.liv` read) | `lucene-codecs/src/live_docs.rs` | ported (read-only), fixture-verified against a real IndexWriter deletion (2 of 5 docs deleted by term, `NoMergePolicy` to keep the segment intact) **m2 sweep (b6-docvalues)**: ghost bits (bits of the last word past `maxDoc`) in a corrupt `.liv` are now reported as `Error::GhostBitsSet` instead of tripping `FixedBitSet::from_words`'s `debug_assert` (a panic in a debug build) or underflowing `max_doc - cardinality()` in a release one. Java asserts the same invariant in `new FixedBitSet(long[], int)`; this port reports it the way every other decode failure in the crate is reported. |
| — `SparseFixedBitSet`/`SparseLiveDocs` in-memory choice | — | not applicable to this port: the on-disk bytes are identical dense bits regardless of Java's in-memory representation choice |
| `codecs/lucene90/Lucene90LiveDocsFormat.writeLiveDocs` | `lucene-codecs/src/live_docs.rs::write` | **write path ported, and genuinely complete for this format** -- unlike every other write-side row in this table, there is no scoped-down subset left to defer. The format itself has no sparse/compressed on-disk variant to omit: `.liv` is always one dense bit array regardless of deletion rate (`SparseFixedBitSet`/`IndexedDISI`-style sparse encodings are Java's in-memory-only choice for `readLiveDocs`, made after the same dense bytes are read off disk -- see the row above), so there's no equivalent of doc-values' "dense only, sparse deferred" or points' "single-leaf only, multi-leaf deferred" caveat here. Real Lucene's `writeBits` copies the input `Bits` in 1024-bit batches via `Bits#applyMask` purely as a performance trick over the generic `Bits` interface; since this port's input is always an already-word-addressable `FixedBitSet`, writing `live_docs.words()` directly produces byte-identical output without needing to replicate that batching. Unit-tested by round-tripping through this port's own reader (`parse`): all-live/no-deletions, some-deleted, a doc count not a multiple of 64 (130 docs, exercising the bitset's partial last-word case), the del-count-mismatch rejection path, and a wrong-segment-id rejection. **Verified against real Lucene in the write→read direction**: `crates/lucene-codecs/examples/write_live_docs_fixture.rs` writes three `.liv` files (`_0`: 5 docs, no deletions; `_1`: 5 docs, 2 deleted; `_2`: 130 docs, 2 deleted including the last doc in the final partial word) and `fixtures/src/VerifyLiveDocs.java` opens each directly through `Lucene90LiveDocsFormat.readLiveDocs` (hand-built `SegmentInfo`/`SegmentCommitInfo`, no `.si`/`segments_N`/`.fnm` writer needed -- deletions aren't per-field, so unlike every other codec write-side fixture here this one needs no `FieldInfos` at all) and confirms every doc's live/deleted state matches the manifest via real `Bits#get(int)` -- all three segments passed on the first real-Lucene run |
| `codecs/lucene94/Lucene94FieldInfosFormat` (`.fnm` read, incl. `FieldInfo.checkConsistency`) | `lucene-codecs/src/field_infos.rs` | ported (read-only), fixture-verified against a real IndexWriter (7 field shapes + a soft-deletes field introduced by a later DV-update generation)  **Sweep b7**: `parse` now also runs the cross-field checks the `FieldInfos(FieldInfo[])` constructor performs -- duplicate field names, duplicate field numbers, more than one soft-deletes field, more than one parent field. Java reaches these from `Lucene94FieldInfosFormat.read` itself (`return new FieldInfos(infos)`), so a `.fnm` tripping one is rejected by real Lucene; previously we accepted it and `field_by_number`'s first-match scan would silently shadow one of two same-numbered fields. Added `field_by_name`, `soft_deletes_field()` and `parent_field()` accessors (`FieldInfos.fieldInfo(String)`/`getSoftDeletesField`/`getParentField`). Still unported and unneeded: the `hasPostings`/`hasNorms`/... aggregate flags, the `byNumber`/`byName` lookup structures, `getMergedFieldInfos`, `FieldInfos.Builder`/`FieldNumbers`, `FieldInfo.verifySameSchema` |
| `codecs/lucene94/Lucene94FieldInfosFormat.write` | `lucene-codecs/src/field_infos.rs::write` | **write path ported.** Always emits the current format version (`FORMAT_DOCVALUE_SKIPPER`); does not re-validate `FieldInfo.checkConsistency` on the way in (callers are trusted, matching the parser's own stance) -- with one exception added by sweep c23: the three indexed-only flags (`storeTermVectors`/`storePayloads`/`omitNorms`) are cleared for a field whose `index_options` is `None`, which is exactly what Java's `FieldInfo` constructor does before `checkConsistency` can see them (`FieldInfo.java:110-114`), and without which a Rust caller could write a `.fnm` that real Lucene coerces on read but this port's own `parse` rejects -- so `check_index` could not open the `.fnm` of a segment `IndexWriter` had just written, and silently skipped every postings check in that segment. It's exercised via own-reader round-trip unit tests instead. **Verified against real Lucene in the write→read direction**: `crates/lucene-codecs/examples/write_field_infos_fixture.rs` writes an `.fnm` file (8 field shapes: plain indexed, term vectors + payloads + an attribute, numeric doc values with a skip index and a gen, sorted doc values, a point field, a KNN vector field, a soft-deletes field, a parent field) and `fixtures/src/VerifyFieldInfos.java` opens it directly through `Lucene94FieldInfosFormat.read` (hand-built `SegmentInfo`, no `.si` writer needed) and confirms every field's properties match the manifest — passed on the first real-Lucene run |
| `codecs/lucene99/Lucene99FlatVectorsFormat` (`.vec`/`.vemf`) + `codecs/lucene95/{OffHeapFloatVectorValues,OffHeapByteVectorValues,OrdToDocDISIReaderConfiguration}` + `codecs/hnsw/{FlatVectorsFormat,FlatVectorsReader,FlatVectorsWriter,FlatVectorsScorer}` + `util/VectorUtil` + `index/VectorSimilarityFunction` | `lucene-codecs/src/vectors.rs::{write_flat_vectors, FlatVectorsReader, FloatVectorValues, ByteVectorValues, OrdToDoc, FloatVectorScorer, ByteVectorScorer, VectorSimilarityFunction::{score,score_bytes}}` | **ported (batch c5), read and write, byte-compatible with real Lucene in both directions.** Replaces the port's own invented `.vec`/`.vem` layout that shipped with task #219. Vectors are addressed by **ordinal**; the ordinal->doc mapping is `OrdToDocDISIReaderConfiguration`'s three cases -- empty (`docsWithFieldOffset == -2`), dense (`-1`, no structures written at all), and sparse (an `IndexedDISI` bitset for doc->ord plus a `DirectMonotonicWriter` sequence for ord->doc, both appended to `.vec` after the field's vectors). `alignOutput`'s 64-byte (FLOAT32) / 4-byte (BYTE) padding is emitted, and the meta's `size * dim * byteSize == vectorDataLength` identity is validated on read. Both `VectorEncoding::{FLOAT32, BYTE}` are covered, with Java's *distinct* byte transforms (`dotProductScore` for byte DOT_PRODUCT, no `max(_,0)` clamp on byte COSINE), and all four `VectorSimilarityFunction`s. `FloatVectorValues` holds a borrow of the mapped `.vec` and decodes into a caller-owned buffer, so a scan allocates nothing (the old reader held `Vec<Vec<f32>>`, one allocation per vector). `mergeOneFlatVectorField` is ported too (batch c10): `FlatVectorsWriter::merge_one_flat_vector_field` streams a field's vectors straight from the segments being merged into the new `.vec` with no decoded intermediate buffer, and copies each unbroken run of surviving ordinals with a **single memcpy** (the on-disk FLOAT32 representation is little-endian `f32` on both sides, where Java re-encodes each vector through a `ByteBuffer`). It assigns merged ordinals in **merged-document order** (batch c22), which is `DocIDMerger.of(subs, mergeState.needsIndexSort)`: with no index sort that is source order and the plan is found already ascending, with one the sources interleave and source order would produce a descending step `.vemf`'s `IndexedDISI` doc list cannot encode. A source whose surviving documents do not map to *strictly increasing* merged ids is rejected outright, since a `MergeState.DocMap` never reorders within a source. **Wired into the segment merge (batch c22)**: `merge::merge_vectors` opens each source's `.vec`/`.vemf` (plus `.vem`/`.vex` when it has a graph), merges the flat store first because that defines the merged ordinal space, then merges the graphs against exactly the bytes just written, and stamps `PerFieldKnnVectorsFormat.format`/`.suffix` on the merged `.fnm` -- zeroing `vector_dimension` for any field that ended up with no vectors, the same rule the flush path applies. A merge is proven to produce **byte-for-byte** what one flush of the same documents would (`a_merge_produces_exactly_what_one_flush_of_the_same_documents_would`). **Not ported**: `VectorEncoding::FLOAT16` and `OffHeapFloat16VectorValues`; `writeSortingField` (index-sort remap -- structurally unnecessary here: both the flush and the merge assign vector ordinals from an already-sorted document order rather than remapping afterwards); `lucene104`'s scalar-quantized formats. **Differential tests**: `fixtures/src/GenVectors.java` writes a real 4000-document, five-field `IndexWriter` segment and `crates/lucene-codecs/tests/vectors_fixtures.rs` reads it back (metadata, spot vectors bit-for-bit, ord<->doc both directions, and Lucene's own brute-force top-k reproduced exactly); `fixtures/src/VerifyVectors.java` + `crates/lucene-codecs/examples/write_vectors_fixture.rs` run the reverse direction through `scripts/verify-write-path.sh`, and one of that fixture's five fields is produced by the **merge** entry points rather than the flush ones, so real Lucene reads and searches merge-written bytes too |
| `codecs/lucene99/Lucene99HnswVectorsFormat` (`.vem`/`.vex`) + `codecs/lucene99/Lucene99HnswVectors{Reader,Writer}` + `util/hnsw/{HnswGraph,OnHeapHnswGraph,HnswGraphBuilder,HnswGraphSearcher,AbstractHnswGraphSearcher,NeighborQueue,NeighborArray,RandomVectorScorer,UpdateableRandomVectorScorer,IncrementalHnswGraphMerger,MergingHnswGraphBuilder,InitializedHnswGraphBuilder,UpdateGraphsUtils}` + `util/{TernaryLongHeap,NumericUtils}` + `java.util.SplittableRandom` + `search/{AbstractKnnCollector,TopKnnCollector}` | `lucene-codecs/src/hnsw_vectors.rs::{write_hnsw_vectors, HnswVectorsReader, OffHeapHnswGraph, search}`, `lucene-codecs/src/hnsw.rs::{OnHeapHnswGraph, HnswGraphBuilder, HnswGraphSearcher, NeighborArray, NeighborQueue, KnnCollector, VectorScorer, merge_graphs, compute_join_set}`, `lucene-util/src/{splittable_random,ternary_long_heap,numeric_utils}.rs` | **ported (batch c5), read, write and search.** `.vex` neighbour lists are sorted, de-duplicated, delta- and group-varint-encoded behind a `DirectMonotonicWriter` node-offset table exactly as `Lucene99HnswVectorsWriter.writeGraph` does (write version `VERSION_GROUPVARINT` = 1; both 0 and 1 are read). Construction ports `M`/`beamWidth` (16/100), the level distribution `(int)(-ln(U) * ml)` with `ml = 1/ln(M)` over a **bit-exact port of `java.util.SplittableRandom(42)`** (which, with `TernaryLongHeap` and the `NumericUtils` float helpers, lives in `lucene-util` -- batch c10 moved all three out of `lucene-codecs`, since none is HNSW-specific in Java and `util/hnsw` has no dependency on `codecs.lucene99`), the `selectAndLinkDiverse`/`diversityCheck` neighbour rule, `NeighborArray.{addAndEnsureDiversity,findWorstNonDiverse,isWorstNonDiverse}`, and `findBestEntryPoint`'s greedy top-down descent. `Lucene99HnswVectorsWriter.shouldCreateGraph`'s tiny-segment skip (`numLevels == 0`, zero-length `.vex` region) is honoured on both sides. **Evidence**: over a real Lucene-written 4000-vector graph, this port's searcher returns Lucene's `KnnFloatVectorQuery`/`KnnByteVectorQuery` results **doc-for-doc, in order**, for 80 queries across four fields; a graph built *here* over the same vectors at the same parameters has the same level count, the same entry node and **recall@10 = 0.9250 against Lucene's 0.9250**; and real Lucene searches a Rust-written graph at recall 0.91-1.00 (`scripts/verify-write-path.sh`). **Merge-time graph reuse is ported (batch c10)**: `hnsw_vectors::merge_one_field` is `Lucene99HnswVectorsWriter.mergeOneField`'s graph half plus `IncrementalHnswGraphMerger.{addReader,createBuilder,getNewOrdMapping,merge}` (including the 40% `DELETE_PCT_THRESHOLD` base-graph rule), and `hnsw::{merge_graphs, compute_join_set}` + `HnswGraphBuilder::init_graph` port `MergingHnswGraphBuilder`, `InitializedHnswGraphBuilder` (structural copy, disconnected-node repair, level rebalance) and `UpdateGraphsUtils.computeJoinSet`. Merging a 1200-vector graph with a 120-vector one costs **11,124 similarity computations against a rebuild's 122,473 (11.0x fewer)**, and merging one undeleted graph reproduces it **arc for arc**. One deliberate divergence: `InitializedHnswGraphBuilder.rebalanceGraph` draws from an **unseeded** `new SplittableRandom()` in Java, so a Lucene merge that hits the deletions branch is not reproducible; this port seeds it. **`SeededHnswGraphSearcher` is ported (M2 sweep `c21-hnsw-seeded`)** as `HnswGraphSearcher::search_seeded`: Java's class exists only to override `findBestEntryPoint` with a constant and delegate `searchLevel`, so it is a second entry point on the one searcher rather than a delegating type. It is reached through `hnsw_vectors::search`'s `SearchOptions::seed_ords`, which is where `Lucene99HnswVectorsReader.search` reads `KnnSearchStrategy.Seeded` off the collector; the same `SearchOptions` also carries Java's `acceptOrds` and `filteredDocCount`, so the filtered, deletions-aware and re-entrant searches all run through the one codec dispatch instead of a copy of it in the search layer. Seeding changes only where level 0's beam starts, never the collector, the accept set or the merge: measured over the 4000-vector fixture at `k = 10` it costs **216 vector comparisons against an unseeded walk's 292 (26% fewer)**, and on the 700-vector leaf the re-entry pass actually re-enters at `k = 100`, 530 against 553 (4.1%). **Not ported**: `FilteredHnswGraphSearcher` (only reachable with a non-default `KnnSearchStrategy.Hnsw` threshold -- `DEFAULT_FILTERED_SEARCH_THRESHOLD` is `0`, i.e. `useFilteredSearch` is never true for `KnnFloatVectorQuery`, so a filtered search walks the plain `HnswGraphSearcher` with `acceptOrds`, which is ported), `ConcurrentHnswMerger`/`HnswConcurrentMergeBuilder` (concurrent merge), `HnswUtil`/`connectComponents` (commented out upstream, apache/lucene#14214), `HnswLock` and the concurrent-build retry loop (this port builds single-threaded), `writeSortingField`. **Measured** (50k x 128, `M=16`, `beamWidth=100`, one core): 26.8k q/s vs 572 q/s brute force (**47x**), 205 distance computations/query vs 50,000 (**244x fewer**), recall@10 = 0.914 on clustered data; graph construction costs **5.5 s** for the segment, against real Lucene's 6.4 s on identical data |
| `codecs/lucene90/Lucene90NormsFormat` (`.nvm`/`.nvd` read: empty/dense/sparse) | `lucene-codecs/src/norms.rs` | ported (read-only), fixture-verified against real per-doc norm values from Lucene's own `NormsProducer`, including a real sparse field (some docs missing it entirely) **c15 sweep**: `norms::validate_fields(&Norms, &FieldInfos)` ports the half of `Lucene90NormsProducer.readFields` that needs `FieldInfos` -- an entry naming a field number the `.fnm` does not have, or a field whose `FieldInfo.hasNorms()` is false, is now a `CorruptIndexException`-equivalent error. `parse_meta`'s signature deliberately still does **not** take `FieldInfos` (23 call sites across four crates, most of them hand-built round-trip tests with no `FieldInfos` to pass); the two places Java's diagnostic actually fires -- `directory_reader.rs`' segment open and `check_index`'s `norms.entries_name_real_norms_fields` -- call `validate_fields` instead, so the behaviour matches even though the signature does not. Reasoning in `docs/sweep/m2/c15-postings-api.md` (b6 #4, c7 F-23, closed). **c19 sweep**: a sparse entry's `docsWithFieldOffset + docsWithFieldLength` is now `checked_add` + `usize::try_from` (`norms::sparse_region`). Both halves are `i64`s read straight off `.nvm` with nothing on the wire relating them, so their sum was an untrusted length that overflowed -- a panic where the caller expected a decode error. Java has no such exposure: `IndexInput.slice(offset, length)` takes the two separately and range-checks each. |
| `codecs/lucene90/IndexedDISI` (sparse doc-id-set: SPARSE/DENSE/ALL blocks) | `lucene-codecs/src/indexed_disi.rs` | **fully ported as Java's stateful cursor** (`DisiCursor`), plus a `decode_doc_ids` one-shot decode kept for the callers that genuinely want an owned doc-id list. `advance_exact` is `IndexedDISI.advanceExact` state for state: `block`/`blockEnd`/`method`/`index`/`nextBlockIndex` across blocks, `exists`/`nextExistDocInBlock` plus an overshoot push-back inside a SPARSE block, and `word`/`wordIndex`/`numberOfOnes`/`denseOrigoIndex` plus `rankSkip` inside a DENSE one -- so a forward walk reads each block's bytes exactly once and a cold random lookup is bounded by one rank read plus `2^(power-6)` words. **M2 sweep `c2-sparse-lookup`** built that cursor and moved `doc_values::NumericReader` onto it: the reader now allocates **nothing** where it held a `Vec<i32>` of every present doc (2.0 MB at 1M docs / 50% density), and lookups got faster at the same time -- a forward scan of a DENSE block ~56x (2.22 ms -> ~39 us for 10,000 lookups), of a SPARSE block ~450x, and a single `NumericReader` lookup on a 100,000-doc field ~1200x (255 us -> ~213 ns). `write_with_dense_rank_power` ports `createRank`, and the doc-values/norms sparse writers now emit the rank table at Java's `DEFAULT_DENSE_RANK_POWER` of 9 (real Lucene reads it: `VerifySparseNumericDocValues` gained three strided passes, the only access pattern that makes Java's `rankSkip` fire, and a deliberately corrupted rank byte fails them). **M2 sweep `c39-codecs-readpath`** closed the last structure: the **block jump table** is now both written (`addJumps`/`flushBlockJumps`, including the empty-block fill and the "a single real block plus the sentinel is wasted space" exemption) and read (`advanceBlock`'s two-blocks-ahead shortcut), with `writeBitSet`'s `jumpTableEntryCount` return value carried into the `.dvm`/`.nvm`/`.vem` entry beside the region's offset and length -- so `docsWithFieldLength` now spans the table, exactly as `createBlockSlice` expects. Real Lucene reads it: `VerifySparseNumericDocValues` gained a block-jump pass (strides of two and three 65 536-doc blocks from 66 starting offsets, the only access pattern that makes Java's `advanceBlock` consult the table at all), verified to fail on a perturbed jump *index* and on a perturbed jump *offset* independently. Measured with an alternating min-of-40 A/B over one build (`crates/lucene-codecs/examples/disi_jump_table.rs`, both arms in one process): 2 000 cold seeks over a 16-block region **158 -> 51 us (3.1x)**, over a 62-block region **458 -> 38 us (12.2x)**, over a 245-block region **2 020 -> 844 us (2.4x)**; a forward scan, which never consults the table, is unchanged to within 0.4%. **M2 sweep `b2-packed`:** `denseRankPower` is validated (`-1`/`0xFF` or `7..=15`, matching both `IndexedDISI`'s constructor and `writeBitSet`) instead of underflowing the `power - 7` shift, and `write` checks its strictly-ascending contract across the whole slice rather than only at block boundaries. **`c43-final-cleanup`**: the block jump table is now read over **Java-written** bytes, which nothing had done -- c39 proved the write side against real Lucene but `IndexedDISI.writeBitSet` emits `jumpTableEntryCount = 0` below two 65 536-document blocks and the largest Java-written index in the tree was 36 000 documents, so there was no table to read. `fixtures/src/GenDisiJumpTable.java` writes 200 000 documents with a DENSE sparse field (every third document, four logical blocks) and a SPARSE one (every 20 000th, last block empty, exercising `flushBlockJumps`' empty-block fill); `crates/lucene-codecs/tests/disi_jump_table_fixtures.rs` cold-seeks through it, scans it, and **perturbs the table Lucene wrote in both its halves** (the index and the offset, independently) to prove the reader consults it. No divergence found -- this closes the one direction of this format that had never been checked, which is where this sweep twice found a writer and a reader agreeing on a shared mistake. |
| `codecs/lucene90/Lucene90DocValuesFormat`/`Lucene90DocValuesProducer` (`.dvm`/`.dvd` read, **all five doc-values types**) | `lucene-codecs/src/doc_values.rs` | ported (read-only), fixture-verified against a real IndexWriter: numeric plain-varying dense, numeric GCD-compressed dense, numeric sparse (`IndexedDISI`), binary fixed-length dense, binary variable-length dense (`DirectMonotonicReader`), binary variable-length sparse (`IndexedDISI` + `DirectMonotonicReader`), a SORTED field with repeated values (ordinal reuse + terms dictionary), a SORTED_NUMERIC field with 0-3 values/doc, and a SORTED_SET field with 0-2 values/doc sharing a terms dictionary (both exercising the sparse-via-`IndexedDISI` and multi-value-via-`DirectMonotonicReader`-address-range paths together). **Varying-bits-per-value block decode (this row's prior gap): ported.** A NUMERIC field split by `Lucene90DocValuesConsumer.writeValues`'s `doBlocks` path into independently-bit-packed `2^blockShift`-value blocks (on disk: `tableSize < -1` in the meta, `blockShift = -2 - tableSize`, `bitsPerValue` written as a meaningless `0xFF` sentinel) now decodes via `NumericEntry::block_shift`/`decode_value_varying_bpv`: each block's own start offset is looked up directly (O(1), no prior-block scan needed) in the per-field jump table at `value_jump_table_offset + block * 8` (an absolute `.dvd`-file offset per block, written by the real encoder), then that block's own header (`bitsPerValue` byte, `i64` delta/constant, and -- when non-constant -- an `i32` packed-byte length) is read and its values bit-unpacked with the same `direct_reader::get` helper the single-width path already uses, reusing the whole field's `gcd` across every block. Previously this was `Error::UnsupportedVaryingBpvBlocks`; that variant is now gone. Fixture-verified: `fixtures/src/GenDocValuesVaryingBpv.java` writes 32,868 docs (two full 16384-value blocks with sharply different value ranges -- block 0 in `0..49`, block 1 in huge widely-spaced multiples of 1e9 -- plus a trailing partial block) through a real `IndexWriter`, forcing the 10%-savings `doBlocks` heuristic to trip, and `crates/lucene-codecs/tests/doc_values_varying_bpv_fixtures.rs` asserts `block_shift.is_some()` (confirming the fixture actually exercises the split, not a silent single-width fallback) and every one of the 32,868 decoded values against real Lucene's own read-back.
