`lucene-search/src/lib.rs::{span_matches_in_doc, span_near_matches, collect_span_leaves, span_doc_ids, span_doc_extents, search_span_query}` | **ported (task #55), scoped to direct in-memory span computation over a doc's already-decoded position lists, not real Lucene's lazy `Spans` iterator API (`nextStartPosition`/`nextDoc`/`advance`, buffered `NearSpansOrdered`/`NearSpansUnordered` merge state) -- see below for the exact scope decision.** `SpanQuery` is a closed three-variant enum -- `SpanTerm { field, term }` (a leaf matching a single term's every occurrence, real `SpanTermQuery`'s exact semantics: each occurrence becomes its own `(position, position + 1)` span, not just "does it occur"), `SpanNear { clauses: Vec<SpanQuery>, slop: u32, in_order: bool }` (real `SpanNearQuery`'s equivalent), and `SpanOr { clauses: Vec<SpanQuery> }` (real `SpanOrQuery`'s equivalent, a pure union) -- wired into a new `Clause::Span(SpanQuery)` variant, resolved via `resolve_clause_docs`/`clause_scores` following the exact `Wildcard`/`Prefix`/`Fuzzy`/`Regexp` precedent: flat, unscored `1.0` per matching doc (real span-aware scoring, `SpanWeight`/`SpanScorer`, is a separate, unscoped problem). **This is a genuinely different query family from `PhraseQuery`** (task #19/#28): a span query's result is the actual matching **span ranges** (`[start, end)` position pairs), not just a doc match/no-match, and those ranges compose (a `SpanNear` of `SpanNear`s, etc). **Scope decision, made explicitly**: rather than porting the lazy `Spans` iterator machinery, `span_matches_in_doc` computes every matching span directly against a doc's already-decoded per-leaf position lists -- the same "compute matches directly against decoded data" shape `phrase_matches_in_doc`/`sloppy_phrase` already use for `PhraseQuery` -- an honestly-scoped MVP: does a doc contain a valid span for this query, and what are its matching span ranges, computed eagerly rather than via a lazy iterator with buffered merge state. `SpanTerm` returns every occurrence as its own span; `SpanOr` returns the sorted, deduplicated union of every sub-query's own spans; `SpanNear` (`span_near_matches`) walked every combination of one span per sub-clause (a cartesian product -- until c43, acceptable for this scope, not the sub-linear early termination a real lazy `NearSpans` iterator gets, same "correctness first, profile before optimizing" call this crate's other multi-term matchers already make) and accepts a combination iff its arranged spans are non-overlapping and increasing with total slack (`sum(next.start - prev.end)` over adjacent arranged spans, generalizing `PhraseQuery`'s Javadoc "moves needed" accounting from single positions to `[start, end)` span ranges -- **not** the same quantity as a sloppy phrase's `matchLength`, which is a window width over slot-shifted positions) at most `slop`. **The `in_order` differentiator, this task's key new capability vs `PhraseQuery`**: `in_order == true` requires the chosen sub-spans non-overlapping and increasing in the query's own clause order (real `SpanNearQuery(clauses, slop, true)`); `in_order == false` is `NearSpansUnordered.atMatch()` in full (`near_spans::unordered_width`): a **pure width test**, `maxEndPosition - top().startPosition() - totalSpanLength <= slop`, with no relative-order requirement *and no non-overlap requirement* -- two clauses may settle on the same span, giving a negative width, which matches. This port rejected the overlap on both arms until **c40**, which dropped hits real Lucene returns (`SpanNearQuery([alpha, alpha], 0, false)` matches every document containing `alpha` at all, including one with a single occurrence); for a non-overlapping arrangement the two branches compute the same number, since `sum(next.start - prev.end)` telescopes to `maxEnd - minStart - sum(lengths)`, so only the overlapping case changed. Ground truth: `fixtures/src/AppendSpanNearManifest.java`'s `spannear.*` hit sets, asserted by `crates/lucene-search/tests/span_query_fixtures.rs::span_near_hit_sets_match_real_lucene` and verified to fail against the pre-c40 rule -- a capability `SpanNearQuery` expresses differently from a sloppy `PhraseQuery`: since c37 a phrase admits reordering too, but its budget is the width of the window over *slot-shifted* positions, where `SpanNearQuery`'s is the summed slack between adjacent spans, and `in_order == true` has no phrase analogue at all (see that row above). `span_doc_ids` (the `Clause::Span` doc-ID resolver) collects every distinct leaf `SpanTerm`'s `(field, term)` pair `query` touches (`collect_span_leaves`), fetches each one's live-filtered position-list map via the same `term_doc_positions` helper `search_phrase_query` uses, and takes the **union of every leaf's own doc list** as a safe, simple over-approximation of the true candidate set (rather than a tighter, per-variant candidate computation, e.g. a `SpanNear`'s candidates could be the conjunction of its sub-clauses' doc lists) -- `span_matches_in_doc` itself already correctly reports "no match" for a candidate doc that doesn't actually satisfy a stricter requirement, so the wider set only costs some wasted lookups, never an incorrect result. Requires `pos_in` whenever `query` has at least one leaf (an `Err(Error::MissingPosInput)` otherwise) -- unlike `PhraseQuery`, even a single-leaf `SpanTerm` needs real per-occurrence positions, so there is no single-term fast path skipping positions the way a length-1 `PhraseQuery` has. **Verified against real Lucene**: `fixtures/src/GenBlockTree.java` gained doc8 (`"pos"` field: `"delta"@0`, `"gamma"@1` -- occurrence order deliberately reversed relative to a `SpanNearQuery` built with clauses in `[gamma, delta]` order) plus `field.pos.span.*` manifest keys, recorded by *actually running* real `org.apache.lucene.queries.spans.SpanNearQuery`/`SpanOrQuery` (the `lucene-queries` module, not `lucene-core` -- these classes are not in `lucene-core-10.5.0.jar`) against this fixture at generation time: real Lucene's own verdict is `SpanNearQuery([gamma, delta], slop=0, inOrder=true)` does NOT match doc8 while `SpanNearQuery([gamma, delta], slop=0, inOrder=false)` DOES -- exactly the `in_order` differentiator this task's own scoping flagged as most likely to be subtly wrong if hand-derived without checking real Lucene, and `crates/lucene-search/tests/span_query_fixtures.rs::span_near_in_order_and_out_of_order_agree_with_real_lucene` confirms this port's implementation agrees with both real-Lucene verdicts (plus a `SpanTerm` sanity check and a `SpanOrQuery` cross-engine check in the same file). Unit tests (`lucene-search/src/lib.rs`) additionally cover: `SpanTerm` matching every occurrence in a multi-occurrence doc; `SpanNear` in-order matching an ordered adjacent pair and rejecting a reversed one; `SpanNear` slop-boundary exactness (exactly-at-limit matches, one-over doesn't); `SpanOr` union semantics (either/neither/both sub-spans matching); nested `SpanNear`-of-`SpanNear` composition (including the no-match-when-inner-fails-to-align case); empty-clauses and missing-leaf edge cases. **Deferred, same as every other `Clause` variant since task #29**: `lucene-ffi` C-ABI construction of a `Clause::Span` clause (the FFI wire format still only builds flat `Clause::Term` clauses; `ffi_search_boolean_query_scored`'s field-name-extraction match now also treats `Span` like `Regexp`/`Fuzzy`/`Wildcard`/`Prefix`/`Phrase`/`Boolean`/`DisjunctionMax`/`ConstantScore`/`Boost`, contributing no field name). A lazy `Spans` iterator API, real span-aware scoring (`SpanWeight`/`SpanScorer`), and the other real span query subclasses this port doesn't cover (`SpanNotQuery`, `SpanContainingQuery`/`SpanWithinQuery`, `SpanFirstQuery`/`SpanPositionRangeQuery`, `SpanMultiTermQueryWrapper`) are all out of scope, tracked here rather than half-implemented. **`c43-final-cleanup`** replaced the cartesian product with the two walks (`near_spans::for_each_ordered_match`/`for_each_unordered_match`), and `combine_span_clauses` is gone with it. Both Java classes are `Spans` iterators whose sub-span cursors only move forward, so the sequence of `(startPosition(), endPosition())` pairs they emit is a strict subset of "every arrangement that fits the budget": `NearSpansOrdered.stretchToOrder` advances each later clause to the first span at or after the previous clause's end and never rewinds, so each position of clause 0 contributes exactly one arrangement -- its class doc says "the formed spans only contains minimum slop matches" -- and `NearSpansUnordered.endPosition()` is the *running* `maxEndPosition`, which can exceed the current arrangement's own maximum end. No hit set moved for a flat `SpanNear` (`span_doc_ids` only asks whether the list is non-empty), which is why this survived three batches; a nested `SpanNear`-of-`SpanNear` **consumes** its inner clause's extents, and there the product returned documents Lucene does not. `span_doc_extents` is the new public shape -- `SpanWeight.getSpans(ctx, Postings.POSITIONS)` walked to `NO_MORE_DOCS`/`NO_MORE_POSITIONS` -- and `span_doc_ids` is it with the extents dropped. Complexity went from exponential in the clause count to linear in the total number of sub-spans. Ground truth: `fixtures/src/AppendSpanExtentManifest.java` records real `Spans` walks for 23 queries (parsed from an S-expression a twin parser in the Rust test reads, so the recorded and the tested query cannot drift) over the committed `multi_segment_scoring_index` -- the only Java-written index whose position lists are rich enough to separate a walk from a product; `crates/lucene-search/tests/span_extent_fixtures.rs` asserts every extent sequence and every nested hit set, both verified to fail against the product. |
| `queries/spans/NearSpansOrdered`/`NearSpansUnordered` (+ `ConjunctionSpans`' positioning) | `lucene-search/src/near_spans.rs::{for_each_ordered_match, for_each_unordered_match}` | **ported (c40-missing-behaviours), over already-decoded spans rather than the lazy `Spans` iterator** -- the same scope decision the `SpanNearQuery` row above records, and the same reason: this port computes a document's matching spans directly from its decoded position lists. `atMatch()` is `SpanTotalLengthEndPositionWindow`'s quantity, `maxEndPosition - top().startPosition() - totalSpanLength`; `for_each_unordered_match` is the walk itself -- `twoPhaseCurrentDocMatches()` followed by `nextStartPosition()` to `NO_MORE_POSITIONS`, a priority queue ordered by `positionsOrdered` (start, then end), advancing the least element and rechecking, with `maxEndPosition` only ever rising as Java's does. **Two consumers**: the *highlighter*'s `phrase_match_offsets` for `slop > 0` (which is what `WeightedSpanTermExtractor` rewrites a sloppy `PhraseQuery` into -- see the `uhighlight` row), and `span_near_matches`' `in_order == false` arm, which now uses the width test rather than a non-overlap rule it never had. **One deliberate divergence, documented on the function**: `positionsOrdered` is not a total order (two spans with the same start and end compare "not less" both ways), so Java's binary heap leaves which of them is `top()` unspecified; the clause index breaks the tie here, which makes the walk deterministic and can only choose between spans the width test cannot distinguish. **Not ported**: `NearSpansOrdered` as a separate module -- its `stretchToOrder` walk is the in-order greedy alignment already inside `phrase_match_offsets`/`span_near_matches`; and the lazy `Spans`/`ConjunctionSpans` iterator API itself. **Verified against real Lucene**: `fixtures/src/AppendHighlightManifest.java` (through `PhraseHelper`) and `fixtures/src/AppendSpanNearManifest.java` (through `IndexSearcher` + `SpanNearQuery`), both over the committed `blocktree_index` fixture with no index regenerated. **`c43-final-cleanup`**: `NearSpansOrdered` is ported here too, as `for_each_ordered_match` -- `stretchToOrder`'s monotone, never-rewinding advance, `matchWidth = sum(start_i - end_{i-1})`, and the "one exhausted sub-span ends the document" early exit. It replaced two hand-written in-order walks (the highlighter's `slop == 0` arm and `span_near_matches`' in-order arm), so the two consumers cannot drift. The standalone `unordered_width` helper is **gone**: it computed `atMatch()`'s formula over one arrangement, which is *not* the quantity the walk tests -- the walk's `maxEndPosition` is a running maximum and its `totalSpanLength` is maintained incrementally -- and keeping a helper that returns a subtly different number is the trap ledger item 8c was. Both callbacks now receive `(arrangement, startPosition(), endPosition())`, which is what a caller composing spans needs. Ground truth for the sequences themselves: `fixtures/src/AppendSpanExtentManifest.java` (see the `SpanNearQuery` row). |
| `search/SortedNumericDocValuesField.newSlowRangeQuery`/`SortedSetDocValuesField.newSlowRangeQuery` (single- and multi-valued), `search/SortField.Type.LONG` (single-key sort), `search/SortedNumericSelector`/`SortedSetSelector` (MIN/MAX) | `lucene-search/src/doc_value_query.rs::{search_numeric_range, search_numeric_range_with_skip_index, search_sorted_ord_range, sort_by_numeric_doc_value, ValueSelector, search_multi_valued_range, sort_by_multi_valued_doc_value}` | **Single-valued NUMERIC/SORTED ported (task #21); multi-valued SORTED_NUMERIC/SORTED_SET range+sort ported (task #31, closing the gap task #21 deferred); skip-index-driven NUMERIC range scan ported (this task, closing task #21/#31's remaining "full sweep only" gap for NUMERIC).** `search_numeric_range` sweeps every live doc in `[0, max_doc)` and reports one matching a `[min, max]` inclusive range on `doc_values::numeric_value` to a `Collector` — the correct fallback for a field with no skip index (this port parses no BKD point index for range queries either, so a full sweep is the only option in that case). **`search_numeric_range_with_skip_index`** is the skip-index-driven sibling: given an already-decoded `doc_values::DocValuesSkipIndex` (`doc_values::parse_skip_index`, task #185), it walks the index's intervals in doc-ID order and skips a whole interval's doc range without decoding a single value whenever that interval's finest-level `[min_value, max_value]` summary can't intersect the query's `[min, max]` (mirrors real Lucene's `PointRangeQuery`-style block-skipping, just over doc-values blocks instead of BKD leaves); only intervals that might match get scanned doc-by-doc. Returns the number of values actually decoded (for callers/tests that want to confirm real skipping happened, not just "didn't crash"). A doc with no value (`numeric_value` returns `None`, legitimate for a sparse field) never matches, including an all-`i64` range, in both functions. `search_sorted_ord_range` is the same shape over a single-valued SORTED field's ordinal (`sorted_ord`); `min_ord == max_ord` is an equality predicate — resolving a term to its ordinal is left to the caller (`terms_dict`). `sort_by_numeric_doc_value` is a standalone post-processing function (not a `Collector` variant — see its doc comment for why: sorting needs the whole candidate set in hand before producing the first output pair, unlike `Collector`'s per-doc streaming callback or `TopDocsCollector`'s incremental top-`N` heap) that takes an already-collected candidate doc-ID list (e.g. `search_term_query`'s output) and returns `(doc_id, value)` pairs ascending by value, ties broken by ascending doc ID (same convention `TopDocsCollector` documents for a score tie); a candidate doc missing a value is either dropped (`MissingValue::Exclude`) or given a caller-supplied substitute (`MissingValue::Default`), both explicit, documented policies. **Task #31's multi-valued addition**: `ValueSelector` (`Min`/`Max`) reduces a doc's multiple values to one comparable value, real Lucene's `SortedNumericSelector.Type`/`SortedSetSelector.Type` equivalent scoped to MIN/MAX only (`MIDDLE_MIN`/`MIDDLE_MAX` are deferred — a niche median-ish reduction with no numeric-field equivalent, documented as a small follow-up in the module doc). `search_multi_valued_range`/`sort_by_multi_valued_doc_value` are the multi-valued siblings of the two single-valued functions above, built directly on the already-read-side-complete `doc_values::sorted_numeric_values` (confirmed to genuinely decode a doc's *entire* multi-valued list, not just one value, making this task tractable without further codec work); both take a `SortedNumericEntry`, which — since `sorted_numeric_values` already reads SORTED_NUMERIC values and a multi-valued `SortedSetKind::Multi` field's ordinals identically — means these two functions serve **both** SORTED_NUMERIC and SORTED_SET's multi-valued case with no separate sorted-set-specific code path (pass the `Multi` variant's `ords` entry for SORTED_SET; a single-valued `SortedSetKind::Single` field still uses `search_sorted_ord_range`, no reduction needed). **Deliberately deferred**: MIDDLE_MIN/MIDDLE_MAX selectors, descending sort/multiple sort fields (single ascending key only), and skip-index-driven range pruning for `search_sorted_ord_range`/`search_multi_valued_range` (SORTED/SORTED_NUMERIC/SORTED_SET — only the NUMERIC path has a skip index to consult today). **Verified against real Lucene**: single-valued functions reuse the already-checked-in `fixtures/data/doc_values_index/` (`varying`/`sparse` fields) and `fixtures/data/sorted_dv_index/` (`sorted` field) fixtures already opened by `crates/lucene-codecs/tests/{doc_values,sorted_doc_values}_fixtures.rs`. **`search_numeric_range_with_skip_index` reuses `fixtures/data/doc_values_skip_index/`** (task #185's fixture, `fixtures/src/GenDocValuesSkipIndex.java`: 36,000 docs, strictly-increasing NUMERIC values, a genuinely 2-level skip index) — differential tests prove it returns byte-for-byte the same matched-doc set as `search_numeric_range` across a narrow single-doc range, a range spanning one base interval, an unbounded match-everything range, and an out-of-domain match-nothing range, plus `live_docs` filtering; separate assertions on the returned decoded-value count prove real interval skipping happens (zero decodes for the match-nothing range, far fewer than 36,000 for the narrow ranges, all 36,000 only for the unbounded range) rather than merely "doesn't crash". The multi-valued functions reuse `fixtures/data/multi_valued_dv_index/` (real `IndexWriter`-written via `fixtures/src/GenMultiValuedDocValues.java`, already checked in and already opened by `crates/lucene-codecs/tests/multi_valued_doc_values_fixtures.rs` for read-side verification, so no new Java generator was needed): a SORTED_NUMERIC `nums` field (values `[5,10],NONE,[7],[1,2,3],NONE` across 5 docs) and a SORTED_SET `tags` field (ords `[0,2],NONE,[1],[0],[1,2]` over 3 terms), both exercising the sparse (`IndexedDISI`) and multi-value (`DirectMonotonicReader` address-range) paths. Fixture-driven tests confirm boundary inclusivity, `live_docs` filtering, missing-doc exclusion under an unbounded range, and — the key selector-specific case — that a doc's MIN falling in range while its MAX doesn't (and vice versa) is decided by the selector alone, not both values; the same range/sort functions are additionally exercised directly against the SORTED_SET `tags` ordinals to confirm they generalize. Also unit-tested in isolation: `ValueSelector::reduce` (MIN/MAX correctness, empty-values case), tie-break-by-ascending-doc-ID, both `MissingValue` policies, empty-candidates, and decode-error propagation. **Swept in `b14-search-features` (m2): CORRECTNESS fix.** `search_multi_valued_range` was documented as `SortedNumericDocValuesField.newSlowRangeQuery`'s equivalent, but real `SortedNumericDocValuesRangeQuery` matches a doc when **any** of its values is in range, with no selector involved -- `SortedNumericSelector` is the *sort-field* reduction (`SortedNumericSortField`). The two disagree on real data (values `[1, 50]` against `[40, 60]`: Java matches, `ValueSelector::Min` does not). Added `search_sorted_numeric_range`, a port of Java's `TwoPhaseIterator.matches()` including its early exit on the first value at or above `lowerValue` and its `MatchNoDocsQuery` treatment of `lower > upper`; `search_multi_valued_range`'s doc now names it as the selector-shaped filter it actually is. |
| `facet/sortedset/SortedSetDocValuesFacetCounts` (basic per-ordinal facet counting only, not the `lucene-facet` module's reader-state/taxonomy machinery) | `lucene-search/src/facets.rs::{facet_counts, resolve_labels, top_n_facets, FacetCount}` | **ported (task #50), single-segment, query-scoped, both scope decisions documented explicitly below.** `facet_counts` takes an already-opened SORTED_SET field's `SortedNumericEntry` (the ordinal-array half — the same shape `doc_value_query.rs`'s multi-valued functions already take for the `Multi` variant of `SortedSetKind`) plus a caller-supplied matching-doc-ID slice, and increments a counter for **every** ordinal of every matching doc (`sorted_numeric_values`, already-decoded — no new format decoding needed, exactly the "thin aggregation layer" this task's brief called for), so a multi-valued doc correctly increments more than one counter, not just a "primary" value. `resolve_labels` turns per-ordinal counts into `FacetCount { ord, label, count }` by walking `terms_dict::TermsCursor` in ordinal order (sweep c38; it used to materialize the whole dictionary through `decode_all_terms` first, which held a second copy of every label beside the result). `top_n_facets` sorts descending by count, ties broken by ascending ordinal (this crate's usual determinism convention, e.g. `sort_by_numeric_doc_value`'s ascending-doc-ID tie-break), then truncates — real Lucene's `Facets.getTopChildren` convention. **Scope decision 1 — single-segment only, not index-wide**: real Lucene's faceting is index-wide because `SortedSetDocValuesReaderState` builds one *merged* ordinal map across every segment's terms dictionary up front; this port has no such merged map, and each segment's SORTED_SET terms dictionary independently assigns its own ordinals (`terms_dict.rs`), so summing raw per-ordinal counts across segments would silently conflate unrelated terms that happen to share an ordinal number in different segments. `facet_counts` therefore operates on one already-opened segment only; a multi-segment caller must merge per-segment results **by resolved string label** (via `resolve_labels`), not by raw ordinal — a straightforward follow-up once per-segment counting is proven correct (which it now is), not implemented here. **Scope decision 2 — query-scoped counting is the only mode**: real Lucene's `FacetsCollector` has no separate "count the whole index" API distinct from running a query that matches everything; `facet_counts` mirrors that by taking an explicit matching-doc-ID slice with no separate all-docs code path — a caller wanting "every doc in the segment" passes every live doc ID as that slice. **Empty-matching-set convention, documented rather than left ambiguous**: an empty matching-doc-ID slice returns a result with every ordinal present at count `0` (a `Vec<u64>` sized to the full terms-dictionary count), not an empty `Vec` — keeps the result's ordinal set always equal to "every term in the dictionary" regardless of match count, so callers never special-case resolving labels for an empty match set. **Verified against real Lucene**: reuses the already-checked-in `fixtures/data/multi_valued_dv_index/` fixture (task #31, `fixtures/src/GenMultiValuedDocValues.java`) — no new Java generator was needed because its manifest already records real ground truth computed via a straightforward per-doc `SortedSetDocValues.nextOrd()`/`lookupOrd()` iteration (`field.tags.ords`/`field.tags.terms` keys), a genuine real-Lucene-computed cross-check without depending on the `lucene-facet` module (not a project dependency) — matching this task's own documented reasoning for why a manual per-doc iteration is an honest substitute. Tests confirm: counts match the manifest's real per-ordinal ground truth exactly (`facet_counts_match_real_lucene_ground_truth`); a multi-valued doc (`tags` = `[red, blue]`) increments both ordinals, not just the first (`multi_valued_doc_increments_every_ordinal_not_just_first`); excluding a doc from the matching set drops exactly its contribution and nothing else (`doc_not_in_matching_set_contributes_nothing`); an empty matching set yields all-zero counts sized to the full term count (`empty_matching_set_yields_all_zero_counts`); `top_n_facets` sorts descending, truncates, and breaks ties by ascending ordinal (three dedicated tests) plus the `n`-larger-than-available and empty-input edge cases; and decode-error propagation from `sorted_numeric_values` surfaces as `Err`, not a panic. `facets.rs` line coverage: 98.74%. **Deferred, matching the two scope decisions above**: index-wide/multi-segment aggregation (label-keyed merge across segments), and any `lucene-facet`-module concept beyond basic per-ordinal counting (hierarchical/taxonomy facets, drill-down/drill-sideways, range facets) — none of which this port has any other supporting machinery for yet either. ~~No `lucene-ffi` exposure~~ — closed by the Faceted search FFI exposure task: see the `lucene-ffi` section's `facets.rs`/`results_facets.rs` row for `ffi_facet_counts_sorted_set`. **Swept in `b14-search-features` (m2): CORRECTNESS + MISSING fixes.** `top_n_facets` kept zero-count facets, which `AbstractSortedSetDocValueFacetCounts.computeTopChildren` never reports. Added `top_children`/`all_children` returning a real `FacetResult` shape (`label_values`/`value`/`child_count`): zero-count children are dropped, ordering is Java's `TopOrdAndIntQueue.OrdAndInt.lessThan` (count DESC, ordinal ASC), `child_count` counts every non-zero child rather than just the surviving top `n`, a `topN` of `0` panics with Java's `validateTopN` message, and a dim with no values in the matched set comes back as `None` -- Java's `null` `FacetResult`, which `getAllDims` filters out -- rather than a list of zeroes. `top_n_facets` is kept and re-documented as the raw ordering primitive, explicitly not `getTopChildren`. **Superseded by c12**, which ported all of it -- `OrdinalMap` (cross-segment ordinals), `FacetsConfig` (so `FacetResult.value` *is* Java's `-1` for a multi-valued dim without `requireDimCount`), `SortedSetDocValuesReaderState` including hierarchical dims, and multi-valued range counting. See the `facet/*` and `index/OrdinalMap` rows below; this row now covers only the per-segment counting primitives (`facet_counts`/`resolve_labels`/`top_n_facets`). Still out of scope: `DrillSideways` and taxonomy faceting. |
| `search/TopFieldCollector` (search-time sort-by-field, numeric single-key only) | `lucene-search/src/collector.rs::{SortDirection, FieldValueDoc, TopFieldCollector}`, `lucene-search/src/doc_value_query.rs::{sort_top_n_by_numeric_doc_value, search_numeric_range_sorted_by_field}` | **ported (task #80).** The first general SEARCH-time "sort matched query results by a doc-value field" collector, distinct from the earlier segment-level index-sort helpers (`sort_by_numeric_doc_value`/`sort_by_multi_valued_doc_value`, task #21/#31, still ascending-only, unchanged by this task). `TopFieldCollector` mirrors `TopDocsCollector`'s exact bounded, always-sorted-`Vec` design (same `O(n)`-per-insert tradeoff), ranking `(doc_id, value: i64)` pairs by `SortDirection::Ascending`/`Descending` instead of `TopDocsCollector`'s score-descending-only order, ties broken by ascending doc ID (same convention). It is deliberately **not** a `Collector`/`ScoringCollector` impl — reading a doc's sort value from doc-values is fallible (`doc_values::Error`), and neither trait's `collect` signature carries a `Result` — so `TopFieldCollector::offer(doc_id, value)` takes an already-decoded, infallible `i64`; callers do the fallible decode themselves. `sort_top_n_by_numeric_doc_value` is the reusable composition point: given any already-collected candidate doc-ID slice (the same contract `sort_by_numeric_doc_value` already has — output of `search_term_query`/`search_boolean_query`/any doc-value range query in this module), a `NumericEntry`, a `SortDirection`, a `MissingValue` policy, and a `top_n` bound, it decodes each candidate via the existing `doc_values::numeric_value` (no new decode logic) and returns the top-`n` `FieldValueDoc`s. `search_numeric_range_sorted_by_field` is a concrete end-to-end wiring of that composition point onto an existing query path: runs `search_numeric_range` into a `VecCollector`, then sorts the matches by a (possibly different) numeric field. **Scope, stated precisely**: numeric doc-value fields only (`SortField.Type.LONG`/`INT` — no `DOUBLE` bit-reinterpretation, no String/`SortedDocValues`-based sort); single sort key only, no secondary `Sort` composition; ties always broken by ascending doc ID, matching every other tie-break convention in this crate. **Missing-value handling**: the same `MissingValue::Exclude`/`Default(i64)` policy `sort_by_numeric_doc_value` already established — `Exclude` drops a candidate with no value from consideration entirely, `Default(v)` substitutes `v` and lets it compete for a rank normally. **Standalone, not wired into every existing scored-search caller**: `search_term_query`/`search_boolean_query`/`search_term_query_scored`/`search_boolean_query_scored` are unchanged; every existing scored-search entry point still returns relevance order only, and a caller wanting field-sorted results calls one of the two new functions above explicitly. **Verified**: 8 new unit tests in `collector.rs` (empty/zero-`top_n`, ascending order, descending order, top-N truncation in both directions, ascending-doc-ID tie-break, `field_rank_order` direct check) plus 8 new unit tests in `doc_value_query.rs` reusing the real, already-checked-in `fixtures/data/doc_values_index/` fixture's `varying`/`gcd`/`sparse` fields — `search_numeric_range_sorted_by_field_end_to_end_real_fixture` queries `gcd in [1000, 1100]` (real values 1000/1025/1075/1200/1050 across docs 0-4, matching docs 0, 1, 2, 4), sorts those matches by the `varying` field (real values -100/7/42/1000/-3), and asserts the exact hand-computed doc-ID order both ascending (`[(0,-100),(4,-3),(1,7),(2,42)]`) and descending (the reverse), plus a top-2-truncated descending case — not just "sorted somehow". A hand-built constant-value case separately confirms the ascending-doc-ID tie-break holds under both directions. `sort_top_n_missing_doc_excluded_by_default_policy`/`_gets_default_value` reuse the `sparse` field (5/NONE/15/NONE/25) to confirm both `MissingValue` policies. `cargo test -p lucene-search`: 442 lib tests pass. `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` clean. **Deferred**: `DOUBLE`/String-field sort keys, multi-key `Sort` composition. ~~`lucene-ffi` C-ABI exposure~~ — closed by the TopFieldCollector FFI exposure task: `lucene-ffi/src/range_sort.rs::{ffi_search_numeric_range_sorted_by_field, ffi_search_numeric_range_sorted_by_field_multi_segment}` (see the `range_sort.rs` row below). **Follow-up (task #88): wired into multi-segment search** — `lucene-search/src/multi_segment.rs::{merge_multi_segment_by_field, search_numeric_range_sorted_by_field_multi_segment, DocValueSegment}` is the sort-by-field analogue of `merge_multi_segment_scored`: runs the existing, unmodified `search_numeric_range_sorted_by_field` independently per already-opened segment, translates local doc IDs to global via `doc_base`, and merges through `TopFieldCollector` a second time, reusing its ascending-doc-ID tie-break rather than reimplementing it. Sequential only — no `rayon`-concurrent sibling yet (unlike the scored path's `merge_multi_segment_scored_concurrent`); adding one without a motivating caller/test would be untested surface, so it's a further follow-up, not silently dropped. Scope otherwise unchanged from `TopFieldCollector` itself (numeric fields, single sort key). Verified by `multi_segment::tests::sort_by_field_multi_segment_translates_doc_ids_across_segments` (two segments with different doc bases, both directions asserted against hand-computed global values) and `sort_by_field_multi_segment_tie_break_is_global_ascending_doc_id` (three equal-valued docs spanning two segments, proving the tie-break survives cross-segment translation). |
| `grouping/CollapsingTopDocsCollector` (field-collapse: keep only the highest-scoring doc per distinct collapse-key value, NUMERIC key only) | `lucene-search/src/collector.rs::{CollapsingCollector}` | **ported, scoped down from real Lucene/Solr's `CollapsingQParserPlugin` collector.** Single-pass algorithm matching real `CollapsingTopDocsCollector` (not two-phase collect-then-rerank): `offer(doc_id, score, key: Option<i64>)` keeps a `HashMap<i64, ScoreDoc>` of one current best-scoring doc per collapse-key value, replacing a group's winner only when a candidate outranks it (reusing `TopDocsCollector`'s own `rank_order` — score descending, lower doc ID wins a tie). `top_docs()` does the final top-`n` reduction over just the group winners. **Scope, stated precisely**: NUMERIC doc-values collapse key only (an already-decoded `i64`), not real Lucene's more common SORTED-field ordinal key — a SORTED key needs an extra ordinal-to-value resolution step (via the field's terms dictionary, as `facets.rs::resolve_labels` already does for a different purpose) not added here; real `CollapsingTopDocsCollector` itself special-cases NUMERIC vs SORTED internally, so this isn't an invented split. Like `TopFieldCollector`, deliberately **not** a `Collector`/`ScoringCollector` impl (the collapse key is a fallible doc-value decode) — callers decode each candidate's key themselves and call `offer` with the already-decoded `Option<i64>`. **Missing collapse-key value (`key: None`)**: kept, not discarded, and **not** collapsed together with other missing-key docs — each survives as its own singleton group, i.e. this port implements Solr's `nullPolicy=EXPAND` (a real, documented policy value), not the `IGNORE` default; chosen as the simpler single case (no separate "drop" path needed alongside "keep, uncollapsed"). **Verified**: 8 new unit tests in `collector.rs` — empty input, `top_n == 0`, multiple docs sharing one key (only the highest score survives), distinct keys all appearing subject to top-N truncation, missing-key docs each surviving independently, group-winner replacement on a better candidate, and the ascending-doc-ID tie-break within a group. `cargo test --workspace` passes; `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` clean. **Deferred**: SORTED/ordinal-keyed collapsing, `nullPolicy=IGNORE`/other Solr null policies, multi-segment wiring (collector-level only, following the same "collector lands first, multi-segment wiring is a documented follow-up" pattern `TopFieldCollector`'s own row above records), `lucene-ffi` C-ABI exposure. |
| `facet/range/LongRangeFacetCounts`/`DoubleRangeFacetCounts` (bucket counting only, not the `lucene-facet` module's reader-state machinery) | `lucene-search/src/facets.rs::{NumericRange, range_facet_counts}` | **ported (task #58), same module and conventions as task #50's SortedSet facet counting above, not a parallel implementation.** `range_facet_counts` takes an already-opened NUMERIC field's `doc_values::NumericEntry`, a caller-supplied `&[NumericRange]` (each with a `label` and independently inclusive/exclusive `min`/`max` bounds), and a matching-doc-ID slice, and returns `(label, count)` pairs in the **same order as the input `ranges` slice** — real Lucene's `FacetResult.labelValues` preserves caller-specified range order, not sorted by count (unlike `top_n_facets`'s deliberate sort for the string-facet case). Reuses `doc_values::numeric_value` (task #21/#31's decode) directly for each doc's value — no new decoding. **Ranges may overlap by design, not by oversight**: real Lucene never requires ranges to partition the value space, so `range_facet_counts` checks every range independently per doc and increments each one whose bounds the doc's value satisfies — a doc matching two overlapping ranges is counted in both, not assigned to a single "winning" bucket. **Missing-value rule matches `doc_value_query.rs`'s existing convention exactly**: a doc with no value for the field (`numeric_value` returns `None`) contributes to no range, including an unbounded-looking one like `i64::MIN..=i64::MAX` on both ends. **Empty-matching-set convention matches task #50's `facet_counts`**: an empty `matching_docs` slice returns every range present at count 0, not an empty `Vec`. **Boundary handling** (`NumericRange::contains`) applies `min_inclusive`/`max_inclusive` independently (`>=`/`>` and `<=`/`<`), so all four combinations are representable. **Verified against real Lucene**: reuses the already-checked-in `fixtures/data/doc_values_index/` fixture (task #21/#31, `varying` field: -100/7/42/1000/-3 across docs 0-4, `sparse` field: 5/NONE/15/NONE/25) — no new Java generator needed, since bucket assignment is hand-verified directly against those already-differentially-verified recorded values rather than re-deriving decode correctness. Tests cover: non-overlapping ranges partitioning the 5 docs correctly; an overlapping-ranges case where doc 2 (value 42) is counted in two overlapping ranges; all four inclusive/exclusive boundary combinations tested precisely at the boundary value 42 itself (`[42,42]` matches, `[42,42)` and `(42,42]` both exclude it, `(7,1000)` still matches 42 while excluding the endpoint docs); the `sparse` field's missing docs never counting under an unbounded range; a doc excluded from `matching_docs` contributing nothing; an empty `matching_docs` yielding all-zero counts; caller-specified range order preserved regardless of count; and decode-error propagation surfacing as `Err`. `facets.rs` line coverage: 99.10% (up from 98.74% pre-task). **Deferred, same scope boundary as task #50**: index-wide/multi-segment range aggregation, and any `lucene-facet`-module concept beyond basic bucket counting (hierarchical/taxonomy facets, drill-down/drill-sideways). ~~No `lucene-ffi` exposure~~ — closed by the Faceted search FFI exposure task: see the `lucene-ffi` section's `facets.rs`/`results_facets.rs` row for `ffi_range_facet_counts`. **Swept in `b14-search-features` (m2): MISSING fix.** `NumericRange` had no constructor, so neither `LongRange`'s validation nor `DoubleRange` existed. Added `NumericRange::new_long` (`LongRange`'s exclusive-to-inclusive `min++`/`max--` normalization plus its `failNoMatch` rejection of an empty range, including a bound already at `i64::MAX`/`i64::MIN`), `NumericRange::new_double` (`DoubleRange`'s NaN rejection and `Math.nextUp`/`nextAfter` nudge **in double space, before** the integer transform, followed by `toLongRange`'s `NumericUtils.doubleToSortableLong`), the `double_to_sortable_long`/`sortable_double_bits` helpers themselves, and `double_range_facet_counts` (`DoubleRangeFacetCounts.count`, applying `mapDocValue`/`sortableDoubleBits` to each stored doc value so it and the range live in the same ordered space). Added `top_range_children`, `RangeFacetCounts.getTopChildren`'s count-DESC/label-ASC ordering with zero-count ranges dropped, `totCount` as `value` and a result even when every range is empty (Java returns a `FacetResult`, not `null`, for the range case). Still single-valued only: `RangeFacetCounts`'s multi-valued `startMultiValuedDoc`/`endMultiValuedDoc` dedup path is not ported. |
| `search/ConjunctionDISI`, `DisjunctionDISIApproximation` (merge algorithms only, not the full `DocIdSetIterator`/`TwoPhaseIterator` class hierarchy) | `lucene-search/src/docid_set.rs::{Conjunction, Disjunction, Excluding}` | **ported as plain `Iterator<Item = i32>` adapters**, not a bespoke `next_doc`/`advance` trait — see the module's doc comment for why `std::iter::Peekable` already covers what real Lucene's `DocIdSetIterator` needs here. `Conjunction` is leapfrog (advance-to-current-max, repeat until all agree), `Disjunction` is a simple per-step min-scan (not a min-heap — acceptable first cut per `rust-performance`'s "correctness first, profile before optimizing"). **That "revisit if clause counts grow" is now done**: the c6 sweep ported `BooleanScorer`'s window/bucket bulk OR as `WindowedDisjunction`, which `matched_boolean_docs` picks over this min-scan for every no-required-clause, 2+-optional-clause query — see the `search/BooleanScorer` row below. `Disjunction` survives for the single-clause case Java's `BooleanScorer` also refuses, `Excluding` is the `must_not` AND-NOT filter. Boxed (`Box<dyn Iterator<Item = i32>>`) rather than monomorphized, since clause count is runtime-determined (`Vec<TermQuery>`) — no closed set of shapes to monomorphize over the way a fixed-arity merge would allow. No `TwoPhaseIterator`/approximation-then-confirm split exists yet (nothing needs it: every clause here is an already-fully-decoded `TermQuery` doc list, not an expensive-to-confirm match). Unit-tested directly (no fixture needed — pure merge logic over synthetic doc-ID lists) for no-overlap/full-overlap/partial-overlap/empty-clause/single-clause/no-clauses cases on both `Conjunction` and `Disjunction`, plus `Excluding`'s exclude-nothing/exclude-everything/exclude-disjoint-from-base/empty-base cases. | **M1.5:** a real leapfrog conjunction now exists for the pure-`must`, all-`Clause::Term` shape (`try_conjunction_lazy`). Cursors are ordered rarest-first by `docFreq` and driven with `LazyDocsCursor::advance`, so per-doc work is O(clauses) rather than O(postings) and no clause's doc list is materialized. Measured 6.3x-16.2x on M1's 5M-doc corpus. Shapes outside that gate (`should`, `must_not`, nested/phrase/wildcard clauses) still fall through to `resolve_clause_docs`, which materializes -- that remains the honest limit. A matching `try_disjunction_lazy` covers the pure-`should` all-`Term` shape, walking the union in lock-step over cursors (6.9x-12.5x measured). It does not prune: every doc in the union is still visited, which is what block-max WAND would fix. Both paths hand `docFreq <= 1` terms back to the general path -- such terms are pulsed into the term dictionary with no `.doc` bytes, so no cursor can be opened for them. **M2 sweep b13:** this module gained the *storage* half of `DocIdSet` it was missing: `RoaringDocIdSet`/`RoaringBuilder` is a full port of `org.apache.lucene.util.RoaringDocIdSet`, all five per-65536-doc-block encodings (all-set, contiguous range, `u16` array up to `MAX_ARRAY_LENGTH` = 4096, the inverse `u16` array for a full block missing fewer than 4096 docs, and a block-local bitset) at Java's exact thresholds, including the builder's buffer-then-upgrade strategy so a sparse block never allocates a bitset. `CachedDocIdSet` is `LRUQueryCache.cacheImpl`'s bitset-vs-Roaring choice (>= 1% density keeps the bitset, `cost * 100 >= maxDoc`) and is what `query_cache.rs` now stores. Measured: 100 documents in a 10,000,000-document space cost 4,928 bytes as Roaring against 1,250,000 as a `FixedBitSet` (254x). `DocIdSetBuilder` remains unported -- no caller: every doc-ID producer in this port already yields an ascending iterator.
| `search/BooleanScorer` (window/bucket bulk OR) + `search/BooleanScorerSupplier.{bulkScorer, booleanScorer, optionalBulkScorer}`'s selection rule | `lucene-search/src/docid_set.rs::WindowedDisjunction`, chosen in `lucene-search/src/lib.rs::matched_boolean_docs` | **ported by the c6 sweep (M2).** Java's `BooleanScorer` walks a `SHIFT = 12`, `SIZE = 4096`-document window at a time: every clause pours its whole run of doc ids into a `FixedBitSet` (`scoreWindowIntoBitSetAndReplay`), plus a parallel `Bucket[]` carrying the per-document clause count when `minShouldMatch > 1`, and the window is then replayed by walking the bitset's words with `Long.numberOfTrailingZeros`. Each clause is read in one long contiguous run and the per-document "which clause is next" decision disappears inside the window. `WindowedDisjunction` is that mechanism over this port's already-materialized per-clause doc-id lists, pull-shaped (an `Iterator<Item = i32>`) so it composes with `Excluding` exactly as `Disjunction` does. Selected the way `BooleanScorerSupplier.booleanScorer` selects it — no required clauses and **more than one** optional clause (`BooleanScorer`'s own constructor refuses fewer than two) — and applied to the `must_not` union as well, that union being the same pure OR. It replaces two things: `Disjunction`'s `O(clauses)` min-scan per emitted document, each step a `peek()`/`next()` through a `Box<dyn Iterator>`; and, for `minimum_should_match > 1`, `should_match_counts`' `HashMap<i32, usize>` tally over the whole segment, now a `u16` at a window-relative index. **Measured** (`crates/lucene-search/benches/boolean_bulk_or.rs`, 1,000,000-document segment): 3.1x on four dense clauses, 9.4x on sixteen, 24.8x with `minimum_should_match = 2`, and 1.07x (no regression) when the clauses are sparse. **Two deliberate divergences**: only non-empty windows are visited (Java's `scoreWindow` picks `top.doc & ~MASK`, so this is the same rule made explicit), and only the word range a clause actually touched is cleared and replayed, where Java pays a fixed 64-word clear per window. **Java's density gate (`costThreshold = maxDoc / 3` when `minShouldMatch > 1`) is deliberately not ported, and the reason matters**: Java's own stated premise — with a minimum-should-match there is no way to know whether the clauses intersect inside a window, so the postings poured in may yield nothing — applies here verbatim. What does not transfer is the *choice* it expresses. Java is choosing against `MinShouldMatchSumScorer`, which **leapfrogs** and can skip postings without reading them; this port's only alternative is `should_match_counts`, which reads every posting of every clause *and* hashes each into a whole-segment map first — worse on every axis at every density, so there is no trade-off left to gate on. Restoring the gate becomes necessary the moment a leapfrogging min-should-match scorer exists here. **Premise correction (this is the finding, not a footnote)**: b12's sweep recorded `BooleanScorer` as the missing mechanism behind this port's scored `or t0 t1 t2 t3` benchmark gap. It is not. `BooleanScorerSupplier.optionalBulkScorer` returns a **`MaxScoreBulkScorer`** (WAND), not a `BooleanScorer`, whenever `scoreMode == ScoreMode.TOP_SCORES` — and `IndexSearcher.search(query, n)` is `TOP_SCORES`, because it builds a `TopScoreDocCollectorManager` with `TOTAL_HITS_THRESHOLD = 1000`, which is not `Integer.MAX_VALUE`. Lucene's own comment says `BooleanScorer` "does not consult score upper bounds and would score every doc in the ... window, defeating top-K pruning". So `BooleanScorer` is Lucene's answer for `ScoreMode.COMPLETE`/`COMPLETE_NO_SCORES` — a disjunction whose hits are all collected or merely counted — and porting it onto the scored top-k path would have been a regression, not the fix. That path's Java counterpart is `MaxScoreBulkScorer`, which this port approximates in `search_boolean_query_scored_maxscore`. **Not ported**: the `needsScores` branch of `scoreWindowIntoBitSetAndReplay` (an `f32` score bucket alongside the clause count, which would replace `clause_scores`' per-clause `HashMap<i32, f32>`), `scoreWindowSingleScorer`'s one-clause-in-this-window fast path, the `DisiWrapper` head/tail priority queues (this port's clause lists are already materialized, so window selection is a min over positions), `ReqExclBulkScorer`, and `filteredOptionalBulkScorer`/`requiredBulkScorer` (both need `Occur.FILTER`, which is unported). **Tested**: `the_windowed_or_matches_the_min_scan_disjunction_on_every_shape` runs eleven shapes x four `minimum_should_match` values against an independently computed brute-force answer *and* against the min-scan it replaces — window boundaries (4095/4096), a clause spanning four windows against one confined to the third, skipped windows, doc id 0, empty clauses, heavy overlap, and the top of the doc-id space (`i32::MAX`, whose exclusive window end overflows `i32`) under a 30-second deadline, because the failure mode there is non-termination rather than a wrong answer. `window_counts_reset_between_windows` pins that a document cannot inherit the previous window's tally. Cross-engine coverage comes from the existing `crates/lucene-search/tests/boolean_query_fixtures.rs`, whose `should_disjunction_matches_real_lucene_union` now runs through this path. |
| `search/WANDScorer`, `ImpactsDISI`, `MaxScoreCache` | `lucene-search/src/lib.rs::{search_term_query_scored_maxscore, search_boolean_query_scored_maxscore}` | **partially ported, scoped narrowly on both the single-term and `BooleanQuery` sides — no full multi-way WAND pivot selection.** `search_term_query_scored`/`search_boolean_query_scored` (the plain scored paths) remain exactly as before: eagerly materializing every matching doc's score with no competitive-score threshold at all. Two separate, narrowly-scoped MAXSCORE-pruned siblings now exist alongside them: <br>- **`search_term_query_scored_maxscore`** (single `TermQuery`, `lucene_codecs::postings::LazyDocsCursor`-backed): once `collector::TopDocsCollector` is holding a full top-`n`, a level-0 block whose `similarity::max_score_for_impacts` upper bound can't beat the collector's current worst kept score is skipped whole (`advance()`d past its last doc, never decoded). Falls back to `search_term_query_scored` verbatim when `doc_in` is `None`, `docFreq <= 1`, or an index option `LazyDocsCursor` doesn't support. <br>- **`search_boolean_query_scored_maxscore`** (BooleanQuery WAND/MAXSCORE dynamic pruning task): a pure SHOULD-disjunction `BooleanQuery` (`must`/`must_not` both empty, `minimum_should_match <= 1`) of plain `Clause::Term` clauses only (no nested `Boolean`/`Phrase`/`DisjunctionMax`/wildcard-family/points clause), each clause's term with `docFreq > 1`. Implements a **simplified two-tier essential/non-essential-style skip, not full multi-way WAND pivot selection** — an explicit, documented scope narrowing (see that function's own doc comment for the full algorithm): each clause gets its own `LazyDocsCursor`, merged by taking the minimum current doc ID across active cursors; before each round, a clause still positioned in a real (non-tail) level-0 block has its own current-block bound (`similarity::max_score_for_impacts`) compared against `threshold - sum(every OTHER active clause's per-clause global bound)`, where a clause's global bound (`idf(doc_freq, doc_count)`, always valid since BM25's `tf_norm` component is strictly `< 1.0` for any finite `freq`) stands in for that other clause's true contribution rather than that other clause's own (potentially block-stale) current bound — strictly more conservative than a fully-refreshed multi-way WAND, never incorrect. Falls back to `search_boolean_query_scored` verbatim the moment any precondition above doesn't hold, or a clause's term doesn't exist/its field doesn't support `lazy_postings`. Both functions share one thread-local skip counter (`test_only_maxscore_block_skip_counter`, `#[cfg(any(test, feature = "test-support"))]`-gated) proving real per-clause block decode is skipped, not just that results match the eager path. **Not yet ported**: `ImpactsDISI`/`MaxScoreCache`-equivalent shared machinery, full multi-way WAND pivot selection for `BooleanQuery` (more than two tiers, continuously-refreshed per-clause block bounds for every clause rather than a global stand-in for "other" clauses), and any dynamic pruning for `PhraseQuery`/`DisjunctionMaxQuery`/nested-`Boolean` scored search. **Multi-segment fan-out (task #213)**: `search_boolean_query_multi_segment_maxscore`/`search_boolean_query_multi_segment_maxscore_concurrent` (`lucene-search/src/multi_segment.rs`) now expose `search_boolean_query_scored_maxscore` across every segment of an index, not just one already-opened segment — see the dedicated row below for scope and FFI exposure. `search_term_query_scored_maxscore` (the single-`TermQuery` side) has **no multi-segment sibling yet** — still single-segment-only. | **M1.5 measurement:** this MAXSCORE implementation is 4-5x *slower* than the plain lazy union that `search_boolean_query_scored` now uses (655ms vs 163ms on `t0 OR t1` over 5M docs; 1,619ms vs 326ms on a four-clause disjunction). It is not failing to prune -- an FFI test proves blocks are provably skipped -- the per-doc bookkeeping simply costs more than the skipping saves at top-k = 50. Left in place rather than bypassed, because disabling it would weaken that invariant test; reviving it means block-max WAND over the same cursors.
| `search/IndexSearcher.search` fan-out + `TopDocs.merge` (matching/scoring fan-out and global merge, no `DirectoryReader` abstraction) | `lucene-search/src/multi_segment.rs::{OpenSegment, merge_multi_segment_scored, search_term_query_multi_segment, search_boolean_query_multi_segment}` | **ported (task #41), scoped precisely below.** Real `IndexSearcher.search` iterates every `LeafReaderContext`, scores/collects each one locally using that segment's own `docBase` to translate local doc IDs to global ones, then merges per-segment `TopDocs` (`TopDocs.merge`/`HitQueue` tie-break: score descending, lower doc ID wins a tie). This port has no `LeafReaderContext`/`DirectoryReader` object to iterate — callers instead pass a plain `&[OpenSegment]` (each entry bundling one already-opened segment's `fields`/`doc_in`/`pos_in`/`pay_in`/`live_docs` plus a caller-computed `doc_base`, the sum of every earlier segment's `maxDoc`). **One shared generic fan-out+merge core** (`merge_multi_segment_scored`), not one copy per query type: every scored query function in this crate already reduces to the same `Vec<ScoreDoc>` shape via `TopDocsCollector`, so the merge step (translate by `doc_base`, re-rank, truncate to `top_n`) needs nothing query-type-specific — `search_term_query_multi_segment`/`search_boolean_query_multi_segment` are thin wrappers around that one core, calling `search_term_query_scored`/`search_boolean_query_scored` per segment; a third query type's multi-segment entry point is another equally thin wrapper, not a second merge implementation. Each segment is collected into its own bounded `TopDocsCollector::new(top_n)` before the cross-segment merge (matching real per-leaf `TopFieldCollector` behavior, and provably lossless: a global top-`top_n` can never need more than `top_n` hits from one segment). The merge itself reuses `TopDocsCollector`'s own comparator a second time (feeding every segment's already-ranked, doc-base-translated hits through one more fresh collector) rather than reimplementing the tie-break rule. **Explicit idf/scoring-parity scope decision — read carefully**: real `BM25Similarity`'s `idf` is computed from `CollectionStatistics`/`TermStatistics` gathered **index-wide** across every segment (`IndexSearcher.termStatistics`/`collectionStatistics`), not per-segment. This port's existing scored query functions (`term_doc_scores`, `lib.rs`, unchanged by this task) compute `idf` from **that one segment's own** `docFreq`/`docCount` (`blocktree::FieldTerms`/`TermStats`) — there is no index-wide statistics aggregation anywhere in this port, and this task does not add one (that would need a `DirectoryReader`-level aggregation step across every segment's term dictionary before scoring starts, out of scope here). Consequently: **this task claims "correct matching + correct per-segment-relative BM25 scoring + correct global merge order," not "byte-for-byte parity with real multi-segment Lucene's BM25 scores"** — whenever a term's `docFreq`/`docCount` genuinely differ across segments (the common case for a >1-segment index), a per-segment idf and an index-wide idf diverge, and this port computes the former. **Verified**: unit tests in `multi_segment.rs` isolate the merge core with synthetic per-segment scores (interleaved/overlapping scores across 2-3 segments, truncation to `top_n`, a zero-match segment, cross-segment tie-break by ascending global doc ID, `top_n == 0`, zero segments, a single segment with nonzero `doc_base`), plus end-to-end calls through both public wrappers against the real `fixtures/data/blocktree_index/` segment. **Cross-engine verification** (`tests/multi_segment_fixtures.rs`): no genuine 2+-segment real-Lucene fixture exists in this repo yet, so — per this task's documented next-best alternative — the same real, `IndexWriter`-produced `fixtures/data/blocktree_index/` segment is opened **twice** (as "segment 0" at `doc_base=0` and "segment 1" at `doc_base=` that segment's own real `maxDoc`), modeling a genuine two-segment index; each "segment"'s real BM25 scores (already fixture-proven by `scoring_fixtures.rs`) are independently concatenated/translated/sorted by this test itself (not by calling the code under test twice) and asserted equal, doc-for-doc and score-for-score, to `search_term_query_multi_segment`'s actual output — proving the merge/doc-base-translation/tie-break logic against real recorded scores, the highest-risk "looks locally correct per-segment, wrong globally" bug class this task's brief specifically calls out. **Still deferred**: real index-wide `CollectionStatistics`-based idf (above); a genuine multi-segment Java fixture generator; multi-segment support for `PhraseQuery`/`DisjunctionMaxQuery`/`ConstantScoreQuery`/`BoostQuery`/`WildcardQuery`/`PrefixQuery` (each would be another thin wrapper around `merge_multi_segment_scored`, not yet added). `lucene-ffi` C-ABI exposure is now closed by task #51 (see its own row below). **`doc_base` computation and per-file segment opening are no longer manual** -- see the `DirectoryReader`/`SegmentReader` row directly below (task #45). | **M1.5:** the per-segment term path now calls `search_term_query_scored_maxscore` (impacts-pruned, `LazyDocsCursor`-backed) rather than `search_term_query_scored` (which materialized every match and scored it). M1 measured the materializing path at 57x slower than Java on a 5M-doc corpus; the swap is 3.3x-7.9x on term queries and is result-preserving -- the full differential suite is unchanged. **M1.6:** the doc-stream read path was swept file by file against the Lucene 10.5.0 source and is now measurably faster than it: `ForUtil.decode` at **2.30x** Lucene's Panama-vectorised decode (median over `bitsPerValue` 1..=31) and posting-list `nextDoc()` at **1.69x**, both via `scripts/bench-micro.sh`, which runs the two engines over identical bytes and joins them. The findings, with before/after numbers for each, are in `docs/sweep/findings.md`; the largest was that `LazyDocsCursor::next_doc` delegated to `advance`, paying a 256-entry binary search per document to compute an offset that is always 1 (7.8x). `bitsPerValue == 32` is decodable by this port and is **not** representable in Lucene (`ForUtil.decodeSlow` indexes `MASKS32`, declared `new int[32]`); this port never writes it. `postings_writer::write_full_block`'s doc-delta encoding choice was corrected to Lucene's condition -- it compared against `num_bit_set_longs * 64` where Lucene compares against `docRange`, so blocks in that band got packed FOR deltas where Lucene writes the unary bit set. **M1-e2e: the deferred index-wide `CollectionStatistics` idf above is now closed.** `multi_segment.rs::global_term_stats`/`global_boolean_stats` sum `docFreq`/`docCount` across every segment before any segment is scored, and thread the result into each per-segment search through `lib.rs`'s `search_{term,boolean}_query_scored*_with_stats` entry points, reproducing `IndexSearcher`'s one-idf-per-reader behavior. The row's "not byte-for-byte parity with real multi-segment Lucene's BM25 scores" caveat therefore no longer applies to `TermQuery`/`BooleanQuery`. It was not a theoretical gap: on M1's 15-segment corpus per-segment idf spread `body:t0` over 0.000473-0.000763 against the global 0.000574, and all 20 benchmark queries returned hit sets that disagreed with Java's -- invisible to every fixture in this repo, all of which are single-segment. **M1.6 extends this to phrase, dismax and boost clauses**, and to `term_doc_scores` -- the path a boolean query takes when its shape does not fit a lazy one, which was still per-segment. The 15-segment benchmark corpus now returns **0 recall mismatches out of 20**, down from 13. **c6 sweep (M2)**: `sumTotalTermFreq`-derived `avgFieldLength` is now aggregated the same way -- `DirectoryReader::avg_field_length` sums `IndexSearcher.fieldStats`' two counters over every leaf and `DirectoryReader::field_norms` builds every leaf's `FieldNorms` from the one value, so the whole `CollectionStatistics` triple is reader-wide, not just the idf half (b13's F-26); and `search_term_query_scored_maxscore_with_stats` no longer drops the reader-wide statistics on its three fallback paths, which had silently reverted a leaf to its own counters whenever the pruned loop declined (b13's F-5, worked around at this module's caller until now -- the guard is deleted). `Clause::Wildcard`/`Prefix`/`Fuzzy`/`Regexp` expand to terms resolved elsewhere and still score per segment.
| `search/WANDScorer` fan-out across `IndexSearcher`'s per-leaf slices (MAXSCORE dynamic pruning, multi-segment) | `lucene-search/src/multi_segment.rs::{search_boolean_query_multi_segment_maxscore, search_boolean_query_multi_segment_maxscore_concurrent}` | **ported (task #213), `BooleanQuery` side only.** Before this task, MAXSCORE dynamic pruning (`search_boolean_query_scored_maxscore`, row above) only existed as a single already-opened-segment operation — a caller with a real multi-segment index (the common case) had no way to get MAXSCORE-pruned search across every segment, in-process or over FFI. `search_boolean_query_multi_segment_maxscore` closes that gap the same way task #41 already closed it for the exhaustive scorer: it is a thin wrapper around the exact same [`merge_multi_segment_scored`] fan-out+merge core `search_boolean_query_multi_segment` uses, with the one line that differs being which per-segment scorer is called (`search_boolean_query_scored_maxscore` instead of `search_boolean_query_scored`) — no new merge/doc-base-translation/tie-break logic, since MAXSCORE pruning is a purely per-segment decision (each segment's block bounds/`docFreq`/`docCount` are its own) that the shared merge core is already agnostic to (it only ever sees each segment's resulting `Vec<ScoreDoc>`, never *how* that segment produced it). `search_boolean_query_multi_segment_maxscore_concurrent` is the `rayon`-based sibling, built on `merge_multi_segment_scored_concurrent` exactly as `search_boolean_query_multi_segment_concurrent` (task #68) already is. Every fallback precondition `search_boolean_query_scored_maxscore` documents (non-empty `must`/`must_not`, `minimum_should_match > 1`, a non-`Clause::Term` should-clause, a term with `docFreq <= 1`, etc.) applies per segment independently — a segment that doesn't qualify falls back to the exhaustive per-segment scorer for that segment alone, with no effect on any other segment or on the final merged/re-ranked result. **No term-query multi-segment MAXSCORE sibling**: `search_term_query_scored_maxscore` (the single-`TermQuery` MAXSCORE path) has **no multi-segment wrapper** in this task — out of scope per this task's brief, which prioritized the `BooleanQuery` case since multi-clause pruning is where MAXSCORE provides the most value; a `search_term_query_multi_segment_maxscore` would be an equally thin follow-up wrapper around the same core, not yet added. **Verified**: `multi_segment.rs`'s own `search_boolean_query_multi_segment_maxscore_matches_exhaustive_and_skips_blocks` opens the real `fixtures/data/blocktree_index/` fixture twice (the same "one real segment opened twice" two-segment simulation task #41's own row documents, since no genuine 2+-segment Java fixture exists in this repo), runs the same three-clause query (`big`/`"everywhere"`, `docFreq == 300`, spanning a real level-0 block plus a tail; `body`/`"cat"`, `body`/`"dog"`) `lib.rs`'s single-segment MAXSCORE tests already use, and asserts the merged output is byte-identical to `search_boolean_query_multi_segment` (the exhaustive multi-segment path) across `top_n` in `{1, 2, 5, 20, 300}` — proving pruning never changes the merged, globally-ranked result. The same test also resets/reads the shared `test_only_maxscore_block_skip_counter` around each call and asserts a nonzero skip count for every `top_n < 300`, proving real per-clause block decode is actually skipped across the two segments, not merely that results happen to match. `search_boolean_query_multi_segment_maxscore_concurrent_matches_sequential` proves the concurrent sibling is byte-for-byte identical to the sequential one, same argument as task #68's own concurrent proof. `lucene-ffi` C-ABI exposure: `ffi_search_boolean_query_multi_segment_maxscore`/`ffi_search_boolean_query_multi_segment_maxscore_concurrent` (`lucene-ffi/src/directory_reader.rs`) — see the FFI row below for the exposure details. |
| `index/DirectoryReader.open(Directory)` + `SegmentReader` (federated segment-opening only, not `MultiTerms`/a unified leaf abstraction) | `lucene-search/src/directory_reader.rs::{DirectoryReader, SegmentReader, OpenedSegments}` | **ported (task #45), scoped precisely below.** `DirectoryReader::open(dir)` finds the latest `segments_N` (new `lucene_index::segment_infos::read_latest`, a thin wrapper over the already-existing `lucene_store::directory::read_latest_commit` + `parse`), opens one `SegmentReader` per listed segment, and computes each segment's `doc_base` as the running sum of every earlier segment's `maxDoc` -- the two things the task #41 row above flags as still manual. Per segment, only the files that segment actually has get opened, checked against `SegmentInfo.files` rather than assumed: `.tim`/`.tip`/`.tmd`/`.doc`/`.pos`/`.pay` are opened together or not at all (a segment with none of them -- e.g. this port's stored-fields-only fixtures, see `segment_writer.rs`'s doc comment -- gets an empty `blocktree::BlockTreeFields::empty()`, a small new constructor added alongside this task); `.liv` is opened only when `del_gen != -1`, reusing `lucene_index::deletes::liv_file_name` rather than re-deriving that filename convention. **Crate placement, worked out explicitly**: this lives in `lucene-search`, not `lucene-index`, because it has to hand back `lucene_search::multi_segment::OpenSegment` values and `lucene-index` cannot depend on `lucene-search` (same downward-dependency constraint already hit by tasks #27/#36). **Two-call API, not one**: `DirectoryReader::open_segments()` returns an `OpenedSegments` that owns the freshly-opened `DocInput`/`PosInput`/`PayInput` values (borrowing from each `SegmentReader`'s own byte buffers), and `OpenedSegments::as_open_segments()` returns the final `Vec<OpenSegment>` borrowing from that -- two calls because `OpenSegment` holds `&'a DocInput<'a>` (a reference to an already-constructed value, not owned bytes), and storing that value directly inside `SegmentReader` would be self-referential, which is illegal without `unsafe` (`lucene-search` is `#![forbid(unsafe_code)]`). **Compound-file segments (`.cfs`/`.cfe`) are now wired in (task #76)**: this is read-path wiring of two *already-existing* primitives, not new codec work -- `SegmentReader::open` checks `SegmentInfo.is_compound_file` and, if set, opens the segment's `.cfs`/`.cfe` pair once (new `CompoundArchive`, `directory_reader.rs`) via `lucene_codecs::compound_format::{parse_entries, check_data_header_footer, open_input}` (unchanged, called exactly as `segment_writer.rs`'s own compound test already does), instead of returning `Error::CompoundFileUnsupported`. Every sub-file lookup (`.fnm`, `.tim`, `.tip`, `.tmd`, `.doc`, `.pos`, `.pay`) now goes through one shared helper, `open_segment_file` (plus `find_segment_file_name` for the name-only lookup `.tim`'s codec-suffix derivation needs), so there is exactly one branch point ("compound: read out of the archive by extension suffix; loose: `dir.open` the `SegmentInfo.files` entry ending in that extension, as before") rather than duplicated ad-hoc logic per file type -- non-compound segments are provably unaffected (same code path, same tests passing). **Verified against a real Java-written compound segment, not just this port's own writer**: `fixtures/data/compound_index/` (`GenCompoundFormat.java`, real `IndexWriter` with `useCompoundFile=true`, 5 docs, a real `.tim`/`.tip`/`.tmd`/`.doc` postings quad packed alongside `.fnm`/stored-fields/doc-values files inside one `.cfs`) now opens successfully through `DirectoryReader::open` and answers a real term query correctly (`compound_file_segment_opens_and_is_queryable`, replacing the old rejection test) -- this is what proves postings-in-a-compound-file also works, not just `.fnm`. A second unit test flushes the same documents through this port's own `segment_writer::flush_stored_only_segment` twice, once loose and once with `use_compound_file: true`, and confirms the compound segment opens with fields/doc-count matching the loose one, with no loose `.fnm` present on disk to fall back to (`compound_flushed_segment_opens_with_field_infos_matching_non_compound_flush`). All prior tests (real single/live-docs fixtures, stored-fields-only, missing-`.fnm`, partial-blocktree, `open_if_changed` reuse/reopen) still pass unchanged. **Still not compound-aware, honestly**: doc-values, norms, and term-vector readers elsewhere in this crate (`doc_value_query.rs`, `field_norms.rs`, `term_vectors_query.rs`, `soft_deletes.rs`) each open their own files directly off the outer `Directory` by name, independent of `DirectoryReader`/`SegmentReader` -- none of them consult `SegmentInfo.is_compound_file` or this task's new `open_segment_file`/`CompoundArchive`, so a compound segment that also had doc-values/norms/term-vectors packed into its `.cfs` would still fail to open through *those* call sites (this port's own write side doesn't yet produce such a segment, so nothing exercises the gap today, but it is real and unclosed). A `MultiTerms`/unified multi-segment `Terms` iteration abstraction and real index-wide `CollectionStatistics`-based idf (task #41's own gaps) remain unchanged by this task. `lucene-ffi` C-ABI exposure of this reader is closed by task #51 (see its own row below). NRT/reopen is closed by task #46 -- see the next row. Soft-delete visibility is closed by task #48 -- see the row below. **M2 sweep b13:** `SegmentReader` now opens the segment's `.nvm`/`.nvd` too (present together or not at all, same contract as `.tim`/`.dvm`, transparently through a `.cfs` archive), parsed via the unchanged `norms::parse_meta` with its `.nvd` header/footer validated, exposed as `norms_entry`/`norms_data` plus a `field_norms(field)` convenience that builds a `FieldNorms` through `from_field_stats` (Java's `sumTotalTermFreq / docCount` from the field's real `.tmd` counters). Before this, norms were unreachable through the reader and every scored multi-segment query opened through it scored at `UNNORMED_FIELD_LENGTH` -- a wrong BM25 length-normalization term, not just a missing feature; on the `blocktree_index` fixture real norms swap the top two hits. `live_docs()` and `soft_deletes_field()` accessors were added alongside. The three per-format codec-suffix derivations (`.tim`, `.dvm`, `.nvm`) are now one `codec_suffix_of` helper. A half-present `.nvm`/`.nvd` pair is `Error::PartialNormsFiles`, not a silent "this segment has no norms" -- the `.tim`/`.tip`/`.tmd` stance rather than the `.dvm`/`.dvd` one, because scoring without norms is silently wrong rather than merely absent. **One recorded divergence**: `field_norms()` derives `avgFieldLength` from *this segment's* `.tmd` counters, where `IndexSearcher.fieldStats` sums them across every leaf, so Java's `avgdl` is reader-wide the same way its `docFreq`/`docCount` are; a `SegmentReader` cannot take that sum, so the reader-wide sibling belongs beside `multi_segment::global_term_stats` and is recorded in `docs/sweep/m2/b13-search-readers.md` (F-26). |
| `index/IndexWriterConfig.setSoftDeletesField` + soft-delete **visibility** (`SoftDeletesDirectoryReaderWrapper`/`PendingSoftDeletes`'s read-side check only, not `IndexWriter.softUpdateDocument`'s write side) | `lucene-search/src/soft_deletes.rs::{SoftDeletesField, is_soft_deleted, is_live, effective_live_docs}` | **ported (task #48), read-side (visibility) only -- see the honest write-side scope call below.** Real Lucene's soft-delete rule is exactly a `DocValuesFieldExistsQuery` over the configured soft-deletes field: a doc is soft-deleted if that field has *any* value for it (presence, not a specific sentinel value). `is_soft_deleted`/`is_live`/`effective_live_docs` implement that same presence-based OR-with-hard-deletes rule against an already-opened `lucene_codecs::doc_values::NumericEntry` (the read side task #4/#21 already built): a doc is invisible if its `.liv` bit is cleared (hard-deleted) **or** its soft-deletes-field doc-values value is present (soft-deleted) -- both conditions independently sufficient, and a doc hit by both stays invisible, not double-counted or contradictory. **Integration point, chosen deliberately**: rather than growing every scored-query function in `lib.rs`/`multi_segment.rs` an extra "and also check this doc-values field" parameter, `effective_live_docs` computes one combined `FixedBitSet` (hard-live AND NOT soft-deleted) that a caller passes straight into any existing `live_docs: Option<&FixedBitSet>` parameter unchanged -- every pre-existing single-hard-delete-only call site is completely unaffected, since nothing forces it to call the new function at all. **Write-side scope decision, now partially closed by task #54**: real Lucene's soft-delete *write* path (`IndexWriter.softUpdateDocument`) relies on `NumericDocValuesFieldUpdates`, which appends a small delta record to an existing segment's doc-values generation rather than rewriting the whole `.dvm`/`.dvd`/`.dvs` triple. At the time this task (#48) was written, this port had no incremental doc-values update format/codec at all -- the only doc-values write primitive (`lucene_codecs::doc_values::write_single_dense_numeric_field`) always wrote a brand-new, complete, single-dense-field triple from scratch, so this task deliberately did not fake a "cheap incremental marking" shim on top of it. **Task #54 has since built exactly that missing incremental-update primitive** (`lucene_codecs::doc_values_updates`, a single-generation sparse `(docId, value)` overlay file, read-through-overlay decode) and wired it into this module as `mark_soft_deleted_via_overlay`/`is_soft_deleted_with_overlay`/`effective_live_docs_with_overlay`: marking one doc soft-deleted no longer requires touching the base `.dvd`/`.dvm` bytes at all. This closes the write-side gap for **one overlay round** -- it does not implement real Lucene's full multi-generation `docValuesGen` lifecycle (many sequential update rounds, newest-generation-wins across rounds) or `SegmentCommitInfo`/`.si` generation-metadata wiring; see `doc_values_updates.rs`'s own doc comment for that scope statement. **Verified**: unit tests cover all four hard/soft-delete combinations (hard-only invisible, soft-only invisible, neither live, both invisible-not-contradictory) plus `effective_live_docs`'s OR-composition (no-soft-deletes-field passthrough, all-live-state, combined-with-existing-hard-deletes, same-doc-both-mechanisms) -- all against the real checked-in `fixtures/data/doc_values_index/` fixture's genuinely sparse (`IndexedDISI`) `sparse` numeric field (task #4/#21's own real-Lucene-written bytes: docs 0/2/4 present, 1/3 absent), not a hand-built or dense-encoded stand-in (a dense field can't represent "no value at all" for any doc, so it can't stand in for a real soft-deletes field's presence semantics). An end-to-end composition test additionally reuses the real `fixtures/data/blocktree_index/` fixture (`body`/`bird` -> docs `[1, 4]`) together with that same real sparse doc-values field to confirm `effective_live_docs`'s output composes correctly with `search_term_query`: doc 4 (soft-deleted per the real doc-values fixture) is excluded from the real term-query match, leaving only doc 1, and additionally hard-deleting doc 1 leaves nothing live at all -- proving the OR-composition against real decoded bytes end-to-end, not just self-consistently. **Deliberately out of scope beyond the write-side gap above**: cross-segment orchestration (like `directory_reader.rs`, this operates on one already-opened segment at a time; a caller wires it in per segment) and `lucene-ffi` C-ABI exposure. **M2 sweep b13:** `effective_live_docs`/`effective_live_docs_with_overlay` now build the bitset in a **single pass** over the field's presence encoding, which is what `PendingSoftDeletes.applySoftDeletes` does (it drains `FieldExistsQuery.getDocValuesDocIdSetIterator` once). They previously looped `0..max_doc` asking `is_soft_deleted` per document, and `doc_values::numeric_value`'s sparse branch rebuilds a `DisiCursor` and walks block headers from the start of the region on every call -- so building live docs was `O(maxDoc x blocks)` where Java pays `O(maxDoc + cardinality)`. The new `present_docs` resolves empty/dense/`IndexedDISI` once; the per-document functions are unchanged for single-lookup callers. Cross-checked against the per-document reference over the real sparse doc-values fixture across five hard-delete patterns x five overlay shapes. The dense ("every doc has a value, so every doc is soft-deleted") and empty-field presence shapes, which the sparse fixture cannot express, are now covered too, along with an out-of-range overlay record and a corrupt presence region. One deliberate divergence, now pinned by a test rather than incidental: Java's `applySoftDeletes` reacts to a `DocValuesFieldUpdates.reset` with `bits.getAndSet(docID)` on the *combined* bitset, so a reset on a hard-deleted document resurrects it; `IndexWriter` never emits that pair, and this port keeps the stronger invariant that a `.liv`-deleted document stays deleted. The reachable half of that branch -- a reset un-deleting a document the base marked soft-deleted -- does match Java and is pinned as well. Corrupt presence metadata is now a typed `Corrupted` error naming the field and the offending value: a negative `numValues` on a dense entry previously became "every document is soft-deleted", i.e. a silently empty segment from one bad byte. **Deliberate divergence (`c41-gates-and-record`)**: `is_live` answers `false` for a doc id that is negative or past the `live_docs` bitset, where Java's `Bits.get` throws. `doc` and `live_docs` reach it as two independent caller-supplied values, and the alternative -- `as usize`, which sign-extends -- was a ghost-bit read or an index panic. `effective_live_docs` likewise bounds against the returned bitset's own length rather than `max_doc`, because `hard_live_bits` clones the *caller's* bitset. See [`docs/mechanical-gates.md`](mechanical-gates.md)'s `fixed-bitset-bound` rule. |
